"Because the chimney was deemed unstable."

"Because the chimney leads to the upper terraces. The ones Corrin wants."

Kael watched her. The wind tugged at the loose strands at her temples. She looked smaller than he remembered, or perhaps he had simply forgotten how much space she took up in a room. The file had listed her height, her weight, the exact measurement of her Kindling resonance. It hadn't listed the way she held herself when she thought no auditor was watching — like a coiled spring, like something about to become airborne.

"Show me the chimney," he said.

She rose in a single fluid motion. "It's a tight squeeze. You'll need to leave the board."

"I'll manage."

The chimney was a vertical slit in the rock, barely wide enough for shoulders. They climbed in silence, Kael following Sol's boots, his fingers finding the same holds she had used. The stone was cold, damp. Thirty feet up, the slit widened into a bell-shaped cavity where a centuries-old rope anchor still hung, its fibers gray and fragile.

Sol braced her back against one wall, her feet against the other. "The shear plane runs horizontal here. The upper block is shifting. Millimeters per year, but the acceleration changed after the winter rains."

Kael wedged himself opposite her, his notebook balanced on one knee. "Acceleration data?"

"Measured. Not reported." She tilted her head back, examining the ceiling. "The anchor point for the binder rods is compromised. If the block slips another centimeter, the whole face goes."

"Then the binder rods need replacement."

"Which requires drilling. Which requires — "

"I know the circle." He wrote: *Shear zone active. Acceleration observed. Binder anchor compromised. Immediate remediation required.* "I'll file the emergency directive today."

"Corrin will appeal."

"Let them. The directive holds pending appeal. That buys us months."

"Months the cliff doesn't have."

He looked up at her. "Then what do you propose?"

She didn't answer. Her gaze had fixed on something above them — a hairline fracture in the ceiling, barely visible in the dim light. Her body went still in a way that had nothing to do with balance.

"Sol?"

"Quiet."

The word was barely a breath. Then the mountain moved.

It started as a vibration in the soles of Kael's boots, a low subsonic hum that traveled up through bone. Dust sifted from the ceiling. A pebble dropped, then another, then a cascade of gravel. The fracture in the ceiling widened with a sound like tearing canvas.

Sol moved before Kael could process the motion. She didn't scramble. She didn't shout. Her hands came up, palms outward, and the air around her shimmered — heat rising off summer pavement, light bending wrong. The Kindling.

Kael had read the theory. He had seen the diagrams. He had never seen it live.

Golden threads erupted from her fingertips, not fire but something stranger — luminous filaments that sought the rock like roots seeking water. They sank into the fracture, into the crumbling binder, into the microfractures spiderwebbing through the ceiling. The glow intensified, shifting from gold to white-blue, and the temperature in the chimney spiked. Kael's skin prickled. His breath caught in a throat suddenly dry.

"Hold," Sol whispered. The word vibrated in the confined space. "Hold hold hold."

The rock groaned. Dust became chunks. A slab the size of a dinner plate broke free and fell past Kael's shoulder, missing his head by inches. He flinched, instinct overriding training, and his shoulder pressed against Sol's arm.

The transfer hit him like a hammer blow to the funny bone — sharp, electric, wrong. His vision whited out at the edges. His right forearm erupted in cold fire, a line of sensation tracing from wrist to elbow in the span of a heartbeat. He gasped, his fingers spasming on the rock, and through the ringing in his ears he heard Sol make a sound — not a scream, something smaller, a broken inhale.

The Kindling flare died as suddenly as it had ignited. The threads retracted into her fingertips. The rock settled with a final heavy sigh.

Kael hung in the chimney, panting, his heart a trapped bird. His right arm felt foreign. He forced his eyes to focus on his forearm.

A faint luminescent line etched itself across the skin, glowing with the same white-blue as the Kindling. It pulsed once, twice, then began to fade, sinking beneath the surface like ink absorbing into parchment. The cold fire resolved into a dull ache, deep in the bone.

"Gods," he breathed.

Sol had gone utterly still. Her face was pale, sweat beading at her hairline. Her eyes were fixed on his arm, on the spot where the mark had been. Her chest rose and fell in shallow bursts.

"Sol." His voice came out rough. "What — "

"I didn't choose you."

The words fell into the chimney like stones into a well.

Kael stared at her. "Choose me?"

"The cost." She swallowed. Her voice cracked. "It transfers to whoever is nearest. I can't direct it. I can only — " She stopped. Her jaw worked. "I can only reduce how much burns. Not who pays."

The chimney felt smaller. The air thicker. Kael looked at his forearm again, at the ordinary skin that had moments ago borne witness to something that should not exist. The Kindling burned years from the wielder. That was the law, the science, the bedrock of the Reckoning. When forced to use it near another, the cost transferred involuntarily. That was the theory, written in the auditor's manuals with clinical detachment.

He had not expected to feel it.

"Years," he said. "How many?"

Her throat moved. "Less than it would have taken from me. I've trained to reduce the magnitude. But I cannot choose the recipient."

"Training." The word tasted of ash. "How long have you been training?"

"Since I was fourteen."

"And your mother?"

"Had no ward. Could not afford one." The words were flat, stripped of defense. "The Reckoning took her because she had no buffer. Because House Corrin prices wards beyond the reach of fallen houses."

Kael's mind raced. The auditor's manual. The Reckoning statutes. The ward trade regulations, all controlled by House Corrin. *Cost transfer is involuntary. Wards are contracted, consenting absorbers. The system provides sanctioned channels for — *

"This isn't sanctioned," he said.

"No."

"You're not a ward."

"No."

"I'm not a ward."

"No."

The wind howled above them, indifferent. Somewhere below, a bird cried.

Kael looked at his arm. The mark was gone, sunk deep. But he felt it now, a phantom weight in the bone, a debt incurred without contract. He was an auditor of the Reckoning. His duty was to observe, to record, to enforce the law. The law said cost transfer without ward contract was illegal. The law said he must report it.

The law also said House Vane's claim was fraudulent, their Kindling unregistered, their lineage-house forfeit.

He had come here to verify the shear zone. He had found something that would destroy them both if it reached the wrong ears.

"Can you climb?" Sol asked quietly.

He realized she had been watching his face. "Yes."

"Then climb. The ledge above is stable. We'll reassess from there."

She moved first, ascending with the same fluid certainty. Kael followed, his right arm weak, his fingers clumsy on the holds. Every movement sent the phantom ache spiraling up his shoulder. He focused on the rhythm: hand, foot, hand, foot. The rock was solid. The rock was real.

They emerged onto the upper terrace where the Vane gardens had once bloomed. Now only hardy weeds grew in the cracked planters. Sol walked to the balustrade and leaned on it, her back to the Reach, facing the cliff face they had just climbed.

Kael joined her. His board was somewhere below, abandoned in the chimney. He would need to retrieve it. He would need to file the emergency directive. He would need to decide what to write in the report about the Kindling flare, about the transfer, about the mark on his arm that was not a ward mark and never would be.

"The shear is worse than the survey," she said. "The upper block has shifted three millimeters since autumn. At this rate, the face fails before the spring equinox."

"Then the emergency directive stands."

"Corrin will appeal. They'll drag it through the courts until the block falls. Then they'll claim the terraces as salvage."

"Salvage law requires abandonment."

"House Vane hasn't abandoned anything. But we can't prove the acceleration without the core sample. Which requires the drill. Which requires the permit."

The circle. It closed every time.

Kael pulled his stylus from his belt. He had a spare notebook in his pack. He wrote: *Shear zone critical. Kindling intervention by subject Sol Vane. Involuntary cost transfer to auditor Kael Ashworth. Magnitude reduced by subject's training. Recipient not a contracted ward. Illegal under Reckoning Statute 47.1.*

His hand shook. The letters wavered.

"What are you writing?" Sol asked.

"The truth."

"All of it?"

He paused. The wind tugged at the page. "The shear zone. The acceleration. The binder failure. The emergency directive."

"And the transfer?"

He looked at her. Her face was expressionless, but her hands gripped the balustrade until the knuckles whitened. "That goes in my private log. Not the official report."

"Why?"

"Because if it goes in the official report, House Corrin gets the legal pretext to seize the lineage-house tomorrow. They'll claim Kindling misuse. They'll claim Vane fraud. They'll install a Corrin ward to 'monitor' you, and that ward will bleed you dry inside a year."

"And if you don't report it?"

"Then I'm complicit in a statutory violation. My commission is forfeit. My house pays the penalty."

She turned. The wind whipped her hair loose from its knot. "So you'll sit on it. Like you sat on the proof about my mother."

Kael went cold. "What proof?"

"Don't." Her voice sharpened. "Don't play the neutral auditor with me. You've had the Corrin ledgers for weeks. The payments to the autumn surveyor. The wire transfers from Corrin's ward-trade account to the Reckoning clerk who filed the stability certification. You know they engineered the failure. You know my mother didn't fail because she was weak. She failed because the system was rigged."

He said nothing. The silence stretched, filled only by wind and the distant cry of gulls.

"When were you going to tell me?" she asked. "At the Reckoning? After the verdict? Never?"

"I don't know what you're talking about."

"Then you're a worse liar than I thought." She pushed off the balustrade. "The inspection is done. The directive will be filed. You'll write your private log. And we'll both pretend this didn't happen."

"Sol — "

"Don't." She walked toward the stair, her boots loud on the stone. "Just don't."

He watched her go. The phantom ache in his arm pulsed in time with his heartbeat. In his pocket, the private log pressed against his thigh, its pages waiting for ink that would condemn him either way.

Above them, the cliff face held. For now.

But the shear zone breathed, and the Kindling slept in Sol's wrists, and somewhere in the Corrin aerie, a ledger balanced on the edge of exposure. Kael touched his forearm, feeling for a mark that no longer showed, and wondered how many years he had just lost to a woman he was sworn to ruin.

---

<!-- chapter_date: 8 Emberfall, Year 3 of the Reckoning Accord -->

The ink had not yet dried on the page when Kael Ashworth realized he had already decided to lie.

His quill hovered over the official auditor's log, the vellum stiff and cold beneath his fingertips. The lamplight in his office at the Reckoning Spire threw long shadows across the stone walls, and somewhere below, the great chains that bound the lineage-houses to the cliff face groaned in the wind. Eight Emberfall. The date sat at the top of the report in his precise hand, the numerals sharp and deliberate.

*Incident Report: Unsanctioned Kindling Manifestation. Location: Lower Terrace, House Vane boundary marker. Time: 23:47, 7 Emberfall.*

He had written the facts first. That was the auditor's discipline. Facts before interpretation. Sequence before consequence.

*Auditor Kael Ashworth, House Ashworth, conducting routine boundary verification pursuant to Article Twelve of the Reckoning Accord. Subject: Isolde Vane, sole claimant of House Vane. Observed unauthorized Kindling deployment at territorial boundary. Manifestation duration: approximately four seconds. Visual confirmation of cost-transfer phenomenon.*

His hand had not trembled. It never did. The Ashworth line bred steadiness into the bone, or so his father said. Valerius Ashworth had held the High Auditor's seat for twenty years before the Accord reshaped the title, and the weight of that lineage pressed against Kael's shoulders every time he signed his name.

He read the next line he had written, the one that made the report official, the one that would be copied into the Spire's archives and forwarded to the Corrin ward-registry within the week.

*Recipient of transferred cost: [REDACTED].*

The brackets were neat. The word inside them was a blade he had sheathed in protocol language. He stared at the redaction until the letters blurred.

Protocol demanded identity. The ward-registry existed to track every soul who absorbed Kindling burn, every contracted ward who consented to the exchange, every coin that changed hands in the Corrin-led trade. An unnamed recipient was a ghost in the ledger. A violation of Article Seven. A gap in the chain of accountability that the Corrin auditors would flag within hours of receipt.

He should have written *Kael Ashworth, Auditor, House Ashworth.* He should have written his own name and accepted the consequence: a formal inquiry, a medical examination by the Spire's physickers, a notation in his permanent record that he had stood too close to a Vane during manifestation. The cost was measurable. The physickers would find the markers in his blood, the faint scarring at his fingertips where years had been shaved away in the space of four seconds. Four seconds. He had counted them in the dark, pressed against the cold stone of the boundary marker, feeling the heat crawl up his arms like something alive.

Four seconds. Approximately three months of natural lifespan. A trivial sum for a man of twenty-eight with no dependents and a career measured in decades. The physickers would confirm it. The inquiry would clear him. The notation would follow him, but notations were the currency of auditors. He had dozens already.

Instead he had written *[REDACTED].*

The quill scratched as he added the next line, the justification he knew no one would accept.

*Redaction authorized under Auditor's Discretion Clause, Article Nine, Section Three: protection of ongoing investigation integrity.*

A lie. There was no ongoing investigation. There was only a woman who had pulled him back from the terrace's edge when the wind shifted, who had burned her own years to stop his fall, who had stood shaking in the aftermath while the transfer marks bloomed across his skin like bruises made of light.

Isolde Vane. Sol.

He had watched her hands afterward, the fingers she pressed to her own wrists as if checking a pulse. She knew what she had done. She knew what it cost. She had not asked his permission. The Kindling did not ask. It took from the nearest living body when the wielder pushed past their own limit, and she had pushed past hers to catch him.

The memory surfaced unbidden: her face in the lantern-light, pale and drawn, the dark hair escaping its tie, the eyes that held his with a mixture of fury and something that might have been fear. *Stay down,* she had said. *Don't move. Let it settle.*

He had not moved. He had lain on the cold terrace stones while the heat worked through him, while his body absorbed what she could not hold, while the years burned clean from his future and settled into his marrow. She had knelt beside him, her breathing ragged, one hand hovering over his chest without touching. Waiting. Watching. Counting.

*Auditor,* she had said when the worst passed. *You'll have to report this.*

*Yes,* he had replied. *I will.*

She had nodded once, sharp and short. *Then report it. But don't you dare tell them it was me who caught you.*

The wind had carried her voice away. She had vanished into the vertical labyrinth of House Vane before the Spire's lanterns could find her, climbing the sheer face with the ease of someone born to the stone. He had watched her go, his fingers pressed to the transfer marks on his forearm, feeling the phantom heat pulse in time with his heart.

Now, in the quiet of his office, the report waited for his signature.

The Ashworth seal sat in its wax warmer, the metal head catching the lamplight. A stylized hawk, wings spread. His father's seal, lent to him when he passed the auditor's examination three years past. *Use it well,* Valerius had said. *The seal is the law made visible. What you stamp, you answer for.*

Kael lifted the quill. His hand formed the name without conscious thought: *Kael Ashworth, Auditor.*

The ink pooled. He set the quill down.

The report was complete. Official. Ready for the morning courier. The redaction would be noticed. Questions would be asked. He would answer them with the clause he had cited, and the Corrin auditors would challenge it, and the matter would escalate to a review board where he would have to defend a decision he could not explain without exposing her.

Exposing her. The phrase tasted of betrayal.

She was a Vane. A fallen house. A claimant whose lineage teetered on the edge of dissolution, whose mother had died in the Reckoning three years past because no ward could be afforded, whose Kindling burned hot and uncontrolled because training required masters House Vane could not pay. She was everything the auditor's oath required him to observe, document, and judge.

She had saved his life.

The transfer marks on his forearm throbbed, a dull ache beneath the skin. He pulled back his sleeve. The marks were faint, a lattice of pale lines tracing the paths where her years had entered him. In the lamplight they looked like frost on glass. Beautiful. Terrible.

Three months. Perhaps four. The physickers would measure it precisely. They would want to know the donor's identity to calculate the burn-rate, to verify the transfer mechanics, to update the ward-registry's actuarial tables. The Corrin trade thrived on precision. Every year absorbed, every coin exchanged, every contract signed in blood and witnessed by an auditor.

Wards were contracted. Consenting. Paid.

He was none of those things.

The Kindling had taken from him without asking. She had not asked. The mechanism did not allow asking. That was the horror of it, the truth House Corrin buried beneath layers of contract law and commercial language: the transfer was involuntary, absolute, and blind. The nearest body paid. Always.

*Unless a ward stands closer.*

The thought rose unbidden, the Corrin logic he had recited a thousand times in hearings and disputes. Wards existed to control the uncontrollable. To place a chosen body between the wielder and the innocent. To make the cost a transaction instead of a theft.

House Vane had no ward. House Vane had never had a ward. The Corrin trade priced wards beyond the reach of fallen houses, and the Accord did not mandate provision. So when a Vane manifested, everyone nearby became an involuntary ward. Everyone paid.

Including auditors conducting routine boundary verification.

Including the son of the former High Auditor.

Kael leaned back in his chair. The stone walls of the Spire office seemed to close inward. The chains below groaned again, a deep metallic sound that vibrated through the floor.

He should amend the report. He should write his name in the brackets and accept the inquiry. The law was clear. The protocol was absolute. Auditors did not redact. Auditors did not protect subjects. Auditors recorded, verified, and witnessed.

*An auditor who cannot be trusted to record the truth cannot be trusted to enforce it.*

His father's voice. A lesson delivered in the Spire's training hall, the echo of a dozen failed candidates. Kael had been nineteen, certain of his place, certain of the law's clarity.

The law was not clear tonight.

He thought of the ward-registry in the Corrin district, the great ledgers where every absorption was logged, every contract filed, every coin tallied. The Corrin auditors would see the redaction. They would demand the name. They would cite Article Seven and the public trust and the integrity of the trade. They would not accept *investigation integrity* from a junior auditor of House Ashworth.

They would investigate him instead.

His career, his house's standing, his father's legacy — all balanced against three months of life he had not agreed to lose, absorbed from a woman who had no right to take them, who had taken them anyway because the alternative was his death on the terrace stones.

*You'll have to report this.*

*Yes. I will.*

He had not promised her what he would write.

The quill sat on the desk, the nib darkened with ink. The report waited. The courier would come at first light. The Spire never slept; the archives operated on a cycle as old as the Accord, and the morning shift would log the report before the sun cleared the eastern ridge.

He had until then.

Kael stood. The chair scraped against stone. He crossed to the window, a narrow arrow-slit that looked out over the Reach. The lineage-houses clung to the cliff face like barnacles, tier upon tier of stone and timber, connected by bridges and stairways carved into the rock. House Vane occupied the lower terraces, the shadowed levels where the wind hit hardest and the sun reached latest. House Ashworth rose above them, pale limestone catching the last light. House Corrin crowned the summit, gilded domes and spires that glittered in the lamplight of a thousand windows.

Somewhere in the shadows of the lower terraces, a woman climbed. She moved through the vertical dark with the certainty of someone who had never known level ground. Her years burned in his blood now, a debt he could not repay, a theft he had agreed to conceal.

The wind carried the scent of pine and cold stone. Below, the river cut a silver line through the valley floor, indifferent to the houses that clung above it.

*Protection of ongoing investigation integrity.*

The lie settled in his chest alongside the transferred years. He could feel them both, twin weights. The investigation did not exist. The integrity was compromised the moment he chose the brackets over the name.

But the alternative — the truth — would bring Corrin auditors to House Vane's door within days. They would examine her. They would measure her Kindling, test her control, calculate her burn-rate and her market value. They would offer a ward contract or a dissolution order. They would strip her of the only thing she owned: the right to decide how her gift was used.

House Corrin owned the ward trade. They owned the contracts. They owned the physickers who certified the absorptions and the auditors who witnessed them. A Vane with uncontrolled Kindling was an asset waiting to be acquired.

His father had taught him that the law protected the vulnerable. The Accord had been written to end the blood-feuds that carved the cliff face into graveyards. The Reckoning existed to test claimants fairly, to measure Kindling against cost, to ensure no house fell through corruption or coercion.

The Reckoning was three months away. The date had been fixed since the first chapter of the Accord. Eighty-seven days until Isolde Vane stood before the auditor's bench and proved her house's right to exist.

Eighty-seven days until the transfer marks on Kael's arm became evidence in a hearing he was honor-bound to preside over.

He turned from the window. The report lay on the desk, the redaction stark in the lamplight. The seal waited in its warmer.

He could burn the report. Write a new one. Name himself. Accept the inquiry. Protect his integrity.

He could send the report as written. Invite the challenge. Buy her time with his credibility.

He could — 

A knock at the door. Sharp. Official.

Kael froze. The courier was not due for hours. The night shift did not disturb auditors unless — 

"Auditor Ashworth." The voice was muffled by oak and iron. "Message from the High Auditor's office. Immediate."

Valerius. His father. The former High Auditor, now senior advisor to the Accord Council, a man who had not spoken to his son in six months except through formal channels.

Kael crossed the room. Opened the door.

A junior clerk stood in the corridor, face pale in the lantern-light, a sealed cylinder in his hand. The corridor stretched behind him, a tunnel of stone and shadow where the Spire's great chains vanished into the mountain's heart.

"Auditor." The clerk extended the cylinder. "The High Auditor requests your presence at first light. There is a matter of protocol requiring your attention."

Kael took the cylinder. Broke the wax. The hawk seal. His father's seal.

The note inside was brief, written in Valerius's precise hand.

*Kael. The Corrin registry flagged an anomaly in last night's boundary reports. An unsanctioned transfer with redacted recipient. You are directed to present your notes and testimony at the seventh hour. Bring the original log. Valerius.*

The paper was thin. Expensive. The kind used for communications that might be preserved.

Kael read it twice. The corridor was cold. The clerk waited, patient, unaware.

"Seventh hour," Kael said. His voice was steady. "I will be there."

The clerk bowed. Turned. Walked away down the corridor, footsteps echoing.

Kael stood in the doorway. The cylinder crushed in his fist. The note burned against his palm.

*Anomaly.* *Flagged.* *Redacted recipient.*

They knew. The Corrin registry had flagged it. Which meant the report had already been copied, transmitted, received. Which meant the courier had come in the night while he sat at his desk wrestling with brackets.

Which meant the redaction had not protected her. It had only marked her.

He returned to his desk. The report sat where he had left it. The ink was fully dry now. The redaction stared back at him, neat brackets around a word that did not exist.

*[REDACTED].*

The first tangible evidence that an uncontrolled transfer had occurred. The first crack in the protocol he had sworn to uphold.

He picked up the quill. The nib caught the vellum.

He could add a line. *Recipient identity withheld pending verification.* He could claim procedural caution. He could buy another day.

But the Corrin auditors would see that addition too. They would know he had amended after the fact. They would know he was hiding something.

The transfer marks on his forearm pulsed. Three months. Four. A debt he had not chosen, owed to a woman who had stolen his fall and given him her future in exchange.

*Stay down. Don't move. Let it settle.*

She had known. She had known exactly what would happen. She had done it anyway.

And he had let her.

The quill hovered. The lamplight flickered. Somewhere in the Spire's depths, the great chains shifted, and the sound rose through the stone like a heartbeat.

Kael Ashworth, Auditor of House Ashworth, son of Valerius, bound by oath and seal and the weight of generations, lowered the quill to the vellum and wrote the only truth he could afford.

*Addendum: The recipient's identity is known to this auditor. Disclosure is withheld under Article Nine, Section Three, pending determination of whether the transfer constitutes a violation of the Accord's protections for non-consenting parties. This auditor accepts full responsibility for the redaction and any consequent findings.*

He signed it. Sealed it. Pressed the hawk into hot wax.

The report was complete. The lie was official. The evidence was recorded.

He placed the document in the outgoing cylinder. Sealed it with his own mark. Set it by the door for the morning courier.

Then he sat in the dark office, alone with the marks on his arm and the clock ticking toward the seventh hour, waiting for his father to ask the question he could not answer: why a Vane's uncontrolled Kindling had burned an Ashworth auditor's years, and why that auditor had chosen silence over the law.

The wind screamed against the Spire's windows. The chains groaned. Somewhere in the vertical dark, a woman climbed toward a dawn that would bring auditors to her door, and the only shield she had was a redacted name in a report that had already been copied, transmitted, and flagged.

Kael closed his eyes. Counted the seconds. Waited for the morning to bring the consequences he had chosen.

---

<!-- chapter_date: 10 Emberfall, Year 3 of the Reckoning Accord -->

The breathing regimen began before dawn, when the cliff face still held the night's chill and the wind moved straight up from the valley floor without obstruction. Sol stood on the narrow ledge behind House Vane's western wing, her boots finding the worn grooves her mother had carved into the stone through decades of the same practice. The Kindling stirred beneath her skin, a low heat in the marrow that responded to altitude and expectation both.

She inhaled through her nose for a count of seven. Held for four. Exhaled through pursed lips for eleven, the air leaving her in a thin, controlled stream that barely disturbed the morning mist.

The heat in her chest did not spike. It rose, gradual as tide, and she met it with the next breath.

Seven. Four. Eleven.

Seven. Four. Eleven.

By the tenth cycle the Kindling's burn had settled into something that resembled a hearth fire rather than a wildfire. She could feel the years waiting at the edges of the gift, the cost that would come due when she finally released the held energy, but the edge had dulled. The ledger had shifted.

Two years per use had been the baseline since she was sixteen. Two years, every time she called the wind to her command, every time she pushed off from stone and let the currents carry her. Her mother had managed one and a half in her prime, or so the family records claimed, though Sol had never seen the proof. The Reckoning that took Mara Vane had come before she could demonstrate the control she swore she was building.

Sol released the breath on the twentieth cycle and let her shoulders drop. The Kindling subsided, retreating to its dormant coil at the base of her spine. She pressed her palm flat against the cold stone of the cliff face and waited for the aftermath.

No graying at the temples. No sudden ache in the joints that marked a year stolen. The fine tremor in her fingers was only fatigue, the ordinary price of holding a difficult position for forty minutes.

One year. Perhaps slightly less.

The knowledge settled in her chest, heavier than the Kindling ever was.

"Again."

Kael's voice came from the shadow of the western wing's doorway. He stood with his auditor's ledger tucked under one arm, his dark coat buttoned against the wind, the silver thread at his cuffs catching the pale light. He had been watching for at least three cycles. She had sensed him before she heard him, the Kindling alert to presence the way a held breath alerts the lungs.

"Twenty cycles is the protocol," she said, not turning. "Three times daily. You've seen the schedule."

"I've seen the schedule." He stepped onto the ledge, his boots sure on the worn stone. "I haven't seen the result. Not measured."

She turned then. The ledger in his hand was bound in Ashworth gray leather, the clasp stamped with the house sigil — a hawk clutching a balance scale. He would write his observations in that book. He would carry them to the Reckoning council in seven weeks and twenty days. Every number mattered.

"One year," she said. "Maybe nine months. I'll need the chronometer to be certain."

He nodded once. The movement was stiff, his jaw tight. "That's half the baseline."

"It's the new baseline."

"Your mother never achieved it consistently." The words were clinical, auditor's language. "The records show she reached fourteen months twice in the year before her Reckoning. Both times under controlled conditions. Never in live exercise."

Sol's fingers curled against the stone. The wind lifted a strand of hair across her cheek. "She didn't have the breathing regimen. She had the theory but not the discipline to hold it."

"She had the discipline." Kael's voice dropped, losing the official cadence. "She had three children and a house falling into debt and a husband who drank the ward fund dry. She had the same twenty-four hours you do. She chose how to spend them."

The air between them thinned. This was the territory they had been circling since the first audit visit three weeks past — the ghost of Mara Vane, the failure that had cost House Vane its standing, the truth that neither of them spoke plainly.

Sol exhaled slowly. "She chose to spend them on us. On keeping the house fed while she trained. The ward fund was empty because she refused to let Corrin's agents place a ward in Vane colors. She said the cost would break whoever took the contract."

"Wards are paid." Kael's hand tightened on the ledger. "Consenting. Contracted. The trade exists because the alternative is what happened to your mother — uncontrolled transfer, no limit on the draw, death by inches."

"The alternative is what happens to whoever stands nearest when I'm forced to use the Kindling without warning." The words came out sharper than she intended. "Like you. Three days ago. On the south face."

Kael went still. The ledger did not move. His face, usually a mask of professional neutrality, flickered — something passing behind the eyes, a calculation or a memory.

"You felt it," she said. Not a question.

"I felt a pull in my left shoulder. Three hours of numbness in the fingers. The physician called it nerve inflammation from the climb." He spoke flatly. "The chronometer on my person recorded a fourteen-month deviation from my projected lifespan. Fourteen months, Sol. In a single incident where you used the Kindling to arrest a fall that would have killed us both."

She had not known the number. She had felt the transfer, the sickening tug of years flowing outward from her center into the only available vessel, but she had not asked for the measurement. Part of her had not wanted to know.

"Fourteen months," she repeated. "From a two-year burn."

"From a burn you couldn't control because the wind sheared unpredictably and you had to choose between catching the ledge and letting us both fall." His voice hardened. "The regimen reduces the baseline. It does not eliminate the transfer. It does not give you choice over the recipient. The mechanic remains what it is — involuntary, uncontrollable, and under Corrin monopoly for anyone who can afford a ward."

"I know what the mechanic is."

"Do you?" He took another step onto the ledge, closing the distance between them. The wind tore at his coat. "Because three weeks ago you told me House Vane's failure was poverty. You said your mother couldn't afford a ward. You didn't say the ward trade is rigged. You didn't say Corrin controls every contract and sets every price and ensures that houses like yours never accumulate enough credit to buy in. You didn't say the system is designed to fail the people who need it most."

Sol's breath caught. The Kindling stirred, a warning heat. "I didn't say it because you're an Ashworth auditor. Because your house profits from the trade. Because telling you would have been handing a weapon to the person holding the ledger that decides whether my house survives the Reckoning."

"Your house survives if the claim is valid." The words were precise, deliberate. "My role is to verify the claim. Nothing more."

"Your role is to rule on the claim. And your father's house owns the ward contracts that make the claim necessary."

The silence stretched, filled only by the wind's constant rush up the cliff face. Somewhere below, a transport lift clanked along its guide rails, carrying ward-contractors to the Corrin exchange at the valley floor. The sound was a reminder of the machinery that turned lives into ledger entries.

Kael's expression shifted. The suspicion that had hardened his features since his arrival softened at the edges, replaced by something that looked uncomfortably like respect — reluctant, measured, the kind an auditor grants a number that balances against all expectation.

"The regimen works," he said quietly. "Half the baseline. Measurable. Repeatable. If you can hold it under stress, under live conditions, it changes the calculus for your Reckoning presentation."

"It changes the calculus for my survival." She met his gaze directly. "The Reckoning is seven weeks away. I need to survive long enough to stand before the council."

"You need to survive the audit first." He lifted the ledger, flipped it open to a fresh page. The scratch of his pen against paper was loud in the quiet. "Three times daily. Twenty cycles. I'll observe each session this week. The council requires documented consistency, not a single demonstration."

"I don't need a witness for my own training."

"You need a witness the council will accept. An Ashworth auditor's notation carries weight." He did not look up from the page. "Unless you'd prefer a Corrin observer. They've requested access three times this month. I've denied them on procedural grounds. The grounds hold until you give me cause to revoke them."

The pen stopped. He looked up, and the respect was undisguised now, mixed with something she could not name — a question, perhaps, or a warning.

"Why?" she asked.

"Because the regimen works. Because your mother's ghost finally has a number attached to it, and that number is better than the one she died chasing." He closed the ledger. "Because I've read every Vane training log from the last fifty years, and none of them show this kind of progress in this timeframe. You've done in three weeks what the records say takes three years."

Sol felt something loosen in her chest, a knot she had been tying since the first audit notice arrived. "The records are incomplete. My mother burned the ones that showed real progress. She said Corrin's spies bought copies from the archivists."

"She was paranoid."

"She was right."

Kael studied her for a long moment. The wind gusted, whipping his hair across his forehead, and he did not brush it away. "The south face incident. The transfer. You knew it would hit me."

"I knew it would hit whoever was closest. You were the only one close enough."

"Three meters. The transfer range is documented at five." His voice was careful. "You could have released the Kindling before you grabbed me. You could have let the fall take you. The burn would have been yours alone."

"And you would have fallen."

"An auditor accepts risk. It's in the oath."

"An auditor also accepts that the subject of the audit is not expendable." She stepped closer, the ledge narrow between them. "The Kindling doesn't care about oaths. It takes from the nearest living body. You were the nearest living body. I chose to catch the ledge because the alternative was watching you die with fourteen months of your life still in you — months you might need for something that matters."

Something flickered across his face again, too quick to name. He looked down at the ledger in his hand, then back at her.

"Three times daily," he said. "I'll be here for the midday session. The evening session. Tomorrow morning."

"You don't need to —"

"I need to." The words were final. "The council requires documented consistency. I'm the only auditor who will write it honestly. The Corrin observers would note the reduction and flag it as evidence of external enhancement — grounds for disqualification under statute seven-three."

"Statute seven-three applies to artificial augmentation. Alchemical, mechanical, ward-assisted." Her pulse quickened. "Breathing is not augmentation. It's discipline."

"Tell that to the council. Tell that to Corrin's advocates. They'll argue that a fifty-percent reduction in baseline burn constitutes proof of undisclosed ward contact, and they'll demand a full physiological audit — invasive, destructive, the kind that ends with your Kindling sealed and your claim voided." He held her gaze. "Unless the Ashworth auditor of record testifies that the progress was observed, documented, and achieved through recognized training methods alone."

The wind howled through the stone corridors of the Reach, carrying the distant clang of the exchange bell. Somewhere a ward-contractor screamed as the transfer took them, the sound thin and distant but unmistakable to anyone who had lived near the Corrin markets.

Sol's hand found the stone behind her, fingers pressing into a groove worn smooth by her mother's palm. The same groove. The same ledge. The same wind.

"She tried this regimen," she said quietly. "In the last month. She wrote it in the log she burned — the one I memorized before the fire. Fourteen months. Twice. She couldn't hold it. The stress of the coming Reckoning, the debt collectors at the door, my father's drinking — she couldn't hold the breath steady enough to keep the Kindling contained."

"And you can."

"I have less to lose." The admission tasted like ash. "No husband. No ward fund to protect. Just the house. Just the claim."

"You have the house. You have the claim. You have seven weeks to prove the regimen holds under examination." Kael tucked the ledger under his arm again. "Midday. Twenty cycles. I'll bring the chronometer."

He turned toward the doorway, then paused. His profile against the brightening sky was sharp, the line of his jaw shadowed.

"Fourteen months," he said, not looking back. "The transfer on the south face. The chronometer shows fourteen months, three days, six hours. I haven't reported it. The audit log shows a nerve inflammation incident, cause undetermined, no Kindling involvement noted."

Sol's breath stopped. "That's falsification. That's —"

"A discretionary notation. The auditor of record has authority to classify incidental medical events that don't bear on the claim under review." His voice was flat, stripped of inflection. "The transfer didn't arise from your claim activity. It arose from a wind shear on a shared climb. The claim concerns your house's lineage rights, not your personal Kindling control. The two are separate in the statute."

"They're not separate. The Kindling is the lineage right."

"The statute disagrees. The statute says the Kindling is a gift subject to regulation. The claim says House Vane's bloodline carries the gift in sufficient strength to maintain the Reach's western watch. Those are different questions." He finally looked at her, and his eyes were the color of the storm clouds gathering over the valley. "I'm not falsifying the record. I'm classifying the event correctly. The transfer was incidental. It doesn't change what your bloodline carries."

"It changes what you carry. Fourteen months. Three days. Six hours."

"An auditor's lifespan is not the council's concern." He moved toward the doorway, his stride eating the distance. "Midday. Don't be late."

The door closed behind him with a solid thunk that vibrated through the stone.

Sol stood alone on the ledge, the Kindling quiet in her chest, the wind resuming its endless conversation with the cliff face. She pressed her palm back into the groove her mother had worn, feeling the ghost of another hand, another breath, another woman who had stood here counting seven-four-eleven until her lungs burned and her vision tunneled and the years slipped away like sand through fingers she could not close.

Fourteen months. Two times. The records said she couldn't hold it.

Sol inhaled. Seven. Held. Four. Exhaled. Eleven.

The heat rose, steady as tide. The ledger in her mind balanced differently now — not two years, but one. Not fourteen months lost to the wind, but fourteen months stolen from the man who had just agreed to lie for her.

The chronometer on his person would tell the truth regardless of what his pen wrote. The Kindling did not negotiate with statutes.

She completed the cycle and began the next, counting the breaths that might save her house, counting the years she would not have to burn, counting the debt she now owed an Ashworth auditor who had just classified a crime as a clerical error.

The question settled in her ribs alongside the Kindling, cold and sharp: what would he classify when the Reckoning came, and the transfer was not incidental, and the years lost were not fourteen months but decades, and the only witness was the man holding the pen?

---

<!-- chapter_date: 11 Emberfall, Year 3 of the Reckoning Accord -->

The patrol had been routine. Three guards from House Vane, two from House Ashworth, moving along the lower ledge where the shale gave way to something harder and older. They carried lanterns instead of Kindling. The Reckoning Accord forbade open flame near the ward stores, and the council's new edict forbade Kindling use without logged justification. Sol had argued for the lanterns. She had won that argument, and the victory tasted like ash.

She heard the crack before she felt the tremor. A dry sound, like bone snapping in a closed fist. Then the ledge shuddered. Dust puffed from the fissures in the cliff face. The lantern light swung wild.

"Hold," Kael said. His voice cut through the settling grit. He stood at the patrol's center, his auditor's badge catching the dim glow. The guards froze. Years of training made them statues.

Sol did not freeze. She moved toward the sound.

The rockfall had taken a thirty-foot section of the path. Two guards were on the far side, waving lanterns through the dust. Three were on her side. One of them, a young Ashworth recruit named Pell, had been caught by the edge. His leg pinned beneath a slab the size of a dining table. He was not screaming. His jaw was locked. His hands clawed at the stone.

Kael was already there. He braced his shoulder against the rock. "Together. On three."

Sol grabbed the opposite edge. The stone was cold. It bit her palms. She smelled iron and old water.

"One. Two. Three."

The slab shifted. A grinding scream from the cliff's throat. Pell gasped. His leg came free, mangled below the knee. Kael hauled him back. Sol followed, boots sliding on gravel.

Then the second crack came.

It started above them. A spiderweb of fractures racing across the overhang. Sol looked up. She saw the mass of it, a slab twice the size of the first, detaching in slow horror. The patrol had seconds. The path behind them was gone. The ledge ahead ended in a sheer drop to the next tier, two hundred feet down.

"Kindling," she said. The word left no room for argument.

Kael's head snapped toward her. His face was gray in the lantern light. "The edict —"

"Is void when the alternative is death." She stepped into the open space between the patrol and the falling rock. Her boots found purchase on the narrow rim. The wind coming up the cliff face tasted of snow and sulfur.

She inhaled. The Kindling answered. It always answered. A coil of heat unfurled behind her sternum, familiar and terrible. She had trained for this. Breath control. Muscle memory. The visualization her mother taught her: a furnace with a regulated damper. She could not stop the burn. She could only narrow the channel.

Her wings manifested. Not feathers. Not membrane. Ribbons of concentrated force, invisible to the eye but palpable as pressure against the skin. They caught the updraft. She rose.

The falling slab met her palms. The impact drove her down six inches. Her knees buckled. The Kindling roared through her veins, consuming the years she had banked, the years she had stolen from sleep and hunger and the quiet moments between heartbeats. She pushed.

The slab arrested. Hovered. Groaned.

She shoved it sideways. It crashed into the cliff face, sending a new shower of debris. The patrol scrambled. Kael hauled Pell. The other guards pulled each other. Sol held the sky.

The burn intensified. Her vision tunneled. She felt the transfer happen — that sickening lurch as the cost sought the nearest vessel. She had hoped, foolishly, that distance might matter. That the patrols' spread might dilute it. She had not accounted for Kael refusing to leave Pell's side.

The first mark had been a hairline fracture across his left pectoral, faint as a scratch from a thorn. This one drove deeper. She felt it through the stone beneath her boots, a resonant vibration in the cliff's bones. Kael did not cry out. She saw his back arch. Saw his fingers gouge the rock as he kept hold of Pell. Saw the lantern fall from his other hand, shattering.

She lowered the slab. Her wings dissolved. The updraft dropped her onto trembling legs.

The patrol was moving. Kael was moving. He had Pell slung over his shoulder, the recruit's broken leg bound with strips torn from a uniform shirt. The other guards formed a cordon. They moved toward the stairwell cut into the cliff, the only way up from this tier.

Sol followed. Her chest burned hollow. The Kindling always took its due. She had lost three months this time. Maybe four. She would not know until she checked her ledger. The ledger she kept hidden beneath a loose floorboard in her room, written in code her mother devised.

Kael set a punishing pace. He did not look at her. He did not speak. The badge on his chest caught the lanterns of the guards ahead, flash flash flash, a rhythm like a heartbeat.

They reached the stairwell. The climb was four hundred steps carved into the living rock. Sol counted them. She always counted. Numbers gave the pain a shape she could hold.

At the second landing, Kael stopped. He set Pell down gently. The recruit's face was wet with sweat and something darker. Kael unclasped his auditor's coat. Let it fall. Pulled his shirt over his head in one fluid motion.

The lantern light revealed the mark.

It cut diagonally from his right shoulder to his left hip. A line of raw, angry red, raised and weeping. Beside it, the old mark — the one from the warehouse district three weeks past — had faded to a silvery thread. The new one crossed it at a sharp angle. An X carved by forces neither of them controlled.

Two marks. Undeniable. Documentable. The kind of evidence an auditor presented to the council to prove Kindling misuse. The kind of evidence that triggered seizure of assets, revocation of lineage rights, the full weight of the Accord.

Sol stood three steps above him. The wind tore at her hair. She could not breathe.

Kael turned. His face was hollowed by pain, but his eyes were clear. They found hers in the dimness. He said nothing. He did not need to. The marks spoke for him. They said: I have been burned twice in your presence. They said: The transfer is real. They said: House Vane's heir is a liability the council cannot ignore.

He pulled his shirt down. The fabric stuck to the fresh wound. He did not flinch. He buttoned his coat. The badge settled over his heart.

"Move," he said to the guards. His voice was rough. "The recruit needs a surgeon."

They moved. Sol moved with them. Her body knew the rhythm. Her mind was elsewhere.

She felt it still. The phantom heat across her own chest, mirroring his. Not imagination. Not sympathy. Something older. The stone beneath her boots had carried the vibration of his pain, conducted it through the cliff's deep roots into her soles, up her legs, into the hollow where the Kindling lived. She had felt the moment the cost struck him. Felt the years tear loose from his future. Felt the silent scream he swallowed.

A bond. That was the word her mother would have used. A resonance. The Kindling recognized its own echo in another body. The cost did not care about house allegiance or auditor's badges or the Accord's careful categories. It sought the nearest life. It found Kael. It would keep finding Kael.

Unless she stopped using the Kindling. Unless she let the next rockfall crush whoever stood beneath it.

The stairwell opened onto the third tier. The surgeon's station was a cave widened by generations of healers, smelling of herb smoke and blood. Kael deposited Pell into waiting hands. He gave orders. The guards dispersed. The lanterns guttered.

Sol remained in the doorway. The cold air bit her sweat-damp skin. She watched Kael wash his hands in a basin of cold water. The red water swirled. He dried them on a cloth. He did not look at his chest. He did not look at her.

"The report," he said. "You will write it. I will countersign. The Kindling use was authorized by auditor discretion. Patrol safety. Standard protocol."

His tone was flat. Procedural. The armor he wore over the wound.

"The patrol was not authorized for Kindling support," she said. Her voice sounded strange to her own ears. Thin. "The edict —"

"Is superseded by the auditor's judgment in life-threatening circumstances. You know this. You wrote the brief for House Vane's compliance office last month."

She had. She had also known he would read it. He read everything.

"Three months," she said. "Maybe four. That's what it took from me."

He turned then. His face was pale. The pain lines around his mouth deepened. "And from me?"

She could not answer. The truth sat in her throat, hot and sharp. The first mark had taken two weeks from him. She knew because she had watched him afterward, cataloguing the changes. The way he tired faster. The way his hands shook when he thought no one looked. The way he checked his own ledger with increasing frequency.

This mark would take more. She did not know how much. The transfer was not proportional. It was chaotic. Greedy. The Kindling took what it wanted from whoever stood closest.

"I will not report the second mark," he said. "Not yet."

The words hung in the herb-smoke air. A breach of his oath. A breach of the Accord. The kind of breach that ended careers. Ended houses.

"Why?"

His gaze held hers. In the dim light, his eyes were the color of storm water. "Because the council would ask how an auditor acquired two Kindling burns in three weeks without filing a single misuse report. Because the answer would lead them to House Vane. Because House Corrin is already circling your claim, and they would use this to accelerate the seizure."

House Corrin. The name tasted like copper. They controlled the ward trade. They held the contracts for every consenting cost-absorber in the Reach. They had bankrupted House Vane by buying the debt on Sol's mother's failed Reckoning, then calling it in at the worst possible moment. Now they wanted the Vane ledge. The mineral rights. The Kindling lineage itself.

"And when they find out anyway?" she asked. "When the surgeon sees? When the guards talk?"

"The surgeon is Vane-contracted. The guards are bound by patrol confidentiality. The report I countersign will state the Kindling use was mine." He said it simply. A lie offered like a gift. "I was demonstrating emergency protocol. You were observing. The cost transferred because I stood too close."

"That is not what happened."

"It is what the record will show." He buttoned his coat to the throat. The badge disappeared. "You will not contradict it. You will not speak of the transfer to anyone. Not your house. Not your trainers. Not the ward brokers. Is that clear?"

The command rung in his voice. Auditor to subject. The power dynamic the Accord had written into their bones.

She should refuse. She should tell him the truth was the only weapon they had against Corrin. She should walk back down those four hundred steps and carve the reality into the council's door herself.

Instead she heard herself say, "Clear."

The word cost her nothing. The truth cost everything.

Kael nodded once. He moved past her, up the corridor toward the Ashworth aerie. His gait was even. He did not limp. He did not favor his left side. The pain was a thing he carried inside the armor, invisible to the world.

Sol stood alone in the surgeon's cave. The herb smoke curled. The basin water grew cold. She pressed her hand against her own chest, over the hollow place. She felt the phantom heat pulse in time with her heartbeat. Two lines crossed on a body that was not hers. An X marking a spot on a map she had not drawn.

The Kindling burned in her veins, quieter now. Waiting. Patient. It had years left to take. It would take them from her, or from whoever stood nearest when she called it forth.

She thought of the warehouse district. The first time. She had been cornered by Corrin's collectors. Kael had intervened. He had stood between her and the contract they waved, the one that would have signed her life to the ward trade. She had flared the Kindling to break the door. He had caught the cost.

She had not asked him to. She had not wanted him to. The Kindling did not ask. It did not want. It simply took.

Now he carried two marks. The council would see pattern. The council would see intent. The council would see an auditor compromised by a fallen house's heir.

And she would carry the silence. She would write the false report. She would watch him check his ledger with increasing frequency. She would feel the phantom heat across her own chest every time the wind blew cold through the Reach.

The bond her mother named. The resonance. It was not love. It was not trust. It was the terrible knowledge that her survival cost him his future, and she would keep surviving anyway.

She turned toward the Vane aerie. The climb was longer from here. The ledge narrower. The wind sharper.

Behind her, the surgeon's cave swallowed Kael's bloody water. Ahead, the cliff face waited. Somewhere in the dark, House Corrin counted their contracts and sharpened their knives.

Sol began to climb.

---

<!-- chapter_date: 13 Emberfall, Year 3 of the Reckoning Accord -->

The dossier arrived before dawn, slipped beneath the door of Kael's chambers with the precision of a blade finding a joint in armor. He found it while pulling on his boots, the wax seal already cracked where his heel had caught the edge. Ashworth crimson, impressed with the house sigil — a hammer crossing a quill, the old auditor's mark. His father's mark.

He broke the seal with a letter opener, the paper giving way with a dry whisper. Inside, three sheets of vellum, each dense with the cramped script of the house scribes. The first listed House Vane's outstanding debts in columns that marched down the page like soldiers: arrears on the cliff-face lease, interest compounded quarterly, penalties for missed Kindling-tithe payments stretching back six years. The total at the bottom carried a red underline, the ink pressed hard enough to dent the fibers.

The second sheet was a market survey. Ward pricing, current as of the last Emberfall equinox. Tier one — consenting absorbers with documented compatibility — commanded twelve thousand crowns per year of expected service. Tier two, untested but contracted, seven thousand. Tier three, emergency conscripts drawn from the lower reaches, three thousand with a mortality clause that released the house from liability if the ward died in harness. The footnotes cited Corrin-controlled registries, each name watermarked with the twin-tower seal.

Kael read the numbers twice. Then a third time, his finger tracking the column for tier one. Twelve thousand crowns per year. House Vane's total debt, including the compounded penalties, came to forty-seven thousand, three hundred crowns. Four years of a tier-one ward. Five if you counted the interest that would accrue while the Reckoning dragged toward its fixed date.

He turned to the third sheet. A single paragraph, centered on the page, in a hand he knew better than his own.

*Kael — The Reckoning approaches. House Corrin moves to consolidate the ward contracts before the audit concludes. If Vane cannot produce a contracted absorber by the hearing, their claim dissolves and the lineage-house reverts to Crown lease. Corrin holds the option on that lease. You know what that means for our position. Resolve this swiftly. The house cannot afford another stalled judgment. — V.*

Valerius Ashworth never signed his notes with more than the initial. He never needed to. The pressure sat in the space between the lines, in the absence of any greeting or farewell, in the word *swiftly* doing the work of a threat.

Kael folded the three sheets with deliberate care, aligning the edges until the creases were sharp enough to cut. He slipped them into the inner pocket of his coat, the one lined with lead-thread to block scrying. His fingers lingered on the vellum a moment longer than necessary.

The chiming bell from the central spire struck six. First light would bleed over the cliff face in twenty minutes, painting the lineage-houses in gold and rust. Sol's house — *Vane's* house — hung three ledges down, its stone ribs dark against the waking sky. He had seen her on the landing platform two days past, wings folded against her back, the Kindling humming beneath her skin like a second heartbeat. She had not looked up. He had not called down.

The transfer had taken three years from him the first time. He knew because he had counted the gray threads in his hair that evening, stood before the mirror in his chambers pulling strands taut against the candlelight. Three years. The scribes said the average first transfer took five. The records said the range was two to eight, depending on proximity and the wielder's control. Sol had control. She had been training in secret for years, the kind of training that left no marks on the official registers. She had held back. She had still taken three.

He sat at his desk and pulled the blank ledger from the bottom drawer. Its cover was plain leather, unmarked, the pages ruled in faint gray lines. He had bought it from a stationer in the lower market three days after the first transfer, paying cash and giving a false name. The shopkeeper had not looked up.

He dipped his pen in ink and wrote the date at the top of the first page. *13 Emberfall, Year 3.* Beneath it, he drew a table with four columns. *Date. Event. Proximity. Years Lost.*

His hand hovered over the first row. The memory surfaced unbidden: the training courtyard behind House Vane's western wing, the scent of pine resin and cold stone, Sol's face illuminated by the Kindling's amber flare as she caught him mid-fall from a collapsed ledge. The gift had roared through her, a wild thing barely leashed, and the cost had sought the nearest vessel. Him. Always him.

*5 Emberfall, Year 3. Forced proximity — training accident. Direct contact. Three years.*

He wrote it in a single breath, the ink bleeding slightly into the fibers. Three years. He thought of the gray threads. He thought of the fine lines at the corners of his eyes that had not been there a week ago, the stiffness in his right knee when he woke, the way his breath came shorter on the long climbs between ledges. Three years stolen from a life that had already been measured in careful increments.

He added a fifth column at the far right. *Cumulative.*

*Three.*

The number stared back at him, black on gray. He capped the pen and set it down, the nib clicking against the inkwell. The ledger snapped shut under his palm.

Outside, the city was waking. The clatter of supply chains on the pulley systems, the distant calls of the ledge-keepers directing traffic, the low rumble of the funicular ascending the cliff face. Normal sounds. The sounds of a world that did not know the Kindling burned time like oil in a lamp, that the light came from someone's future.

Kael stood and moved to the window. The glass was thick, warped at the edges from the heat of the forge-fires that warmed the house's lower levels. Through it, he could see the full span of Thornmere Reach — the great houses clinging to the rock like barnacles, the bridges spanning the chasms, the tiny figures moving along the narrow paths. Somewhere down there, Sol was waking. Perhaps she was already on the platform, testing her wings. Perhaps she was reading the summons he had sent yesterday, the formal auditor's notice requiring her presence at the preliminary hearing in seven days.

Seven days. The Reckoning was fixed for the winter solstice, sixty-seven days from now. The preliminary hearing was a formality, a procedural gate that Kael controlled. He could delay it. He could accelerate it. He could shape the terms of Vane's defense with a word, a ruling, a selective reading of the debt statutes.

His father's note burned in his pocket. *Resolve this swiftly.*

The house could not afford another stalled judgment. The words meant Corrin was moving. The twin-tower seal on the ward registry footnotes meant Corrin was already moving. If Vane fell, Corrin took the lease. If Corrin took the lease, Ashworth lost the audit fees, the tithe percentages, the quiet influence that came from being the neutral hand on the scales. Valerius had built that influence over three decades. He would not let it crumble for a fallen house and a girl who burned time like currency.

Kael's reflection watched him from the warped glass. Dark hair, graying at the temples. Hazel eyes, the left one slightly lighter than the right — a birth quirk, not a Kindling mark. The lines around his mouth deeper than they had been a month ago. Three years. The ledger said three. His body knew the truth of it.

He turned from the window and began to dress for the day. The auditor's robes hung in the wardrobe, charcoal wool with the silver thread at the cuffs. He pulled them on, fastened the clasps, pinned the badge of office at his throat — the balanced scales, the quill crossed by the hammer. The weight settled on his shoulders, familiar and heavy.

At the door, he paused. The ledger sat on the desk, a plain leather book among the official folios. Anyone searching his chambers would see it, might open it, might read the single entry in his careful script. He should hide it. Burn it. Commit the numbers to memory and destroy the evidence.

He left it where it lay.

The corridor outside was empty, the stone floor cold through the soles of his boots. He walked toward the central stair, the one that descended to the audit chambers. Behind him, the first full light of Emberfall struck the window, illuminating the ledger's cover for a moment — plain leather, unmarked, holding a debt that no contract could explain.

He did not look back. The hearing was in seven days. The Reckoning in sixty-seven. The ledger had one entry. He knew, with a certainty that settled in his bones like frost, that it would not be the last.

---

The dossier sat in the center of Kael's desk, its leather cover worn smooth by generations of auditors' hands. He had read it three times already. A fourth reading would not change what the ink said, but his fingers kept returning to the first page as if the texture might yield a different truth.

House Vane. Claimant: Isolde Vane, sole surviving heir. Asset: one Kindling bloodline, attested by the Reckoning Accord's founding charter. Liability: no contracted ward. The words were clinical, stripped of the weight they carried. He had seen the same phrasing on a dozen other files. The Accord's language did not bend for tragedy.

He turned to the technical appendix, the section most auditors skimmed. The Kindling's burn rate. The transfer mechanics. The ward efficacy thresholds. This was where the numbers lived, and numbers did not lie.

A contracted ward, the text read, absorbs ninety-seven percent of the cost transferred during involuntary proximity events. The remaining three percent bleeds to the nearest living body within a five-foot radius. The bleed is unavoidable. No technique, no ward contract, no bloodline variation has ever altered this constant.

Kael read the sentence again. Then a third time. The ink did not blur. The percentage did not shift.

Three percent. It sounded small. A rounding error in a ledger. But three percent of a Kindling wielder's remaining years, transferred involuntarily to whoever stood closest — that was not a rounding error. That was a sentence served in installments.

He thought of the night on the eastern ledge: the flare of Sol's wings catching the lantern light, the way the air had grown thin and cold. He stood within three feet. A chill pierced him, deeper than the wind, and the edges of his vision grayed for a moment. Exhaustion, altitude, the stress of the audit — he had blamed all three at the time. His proximity report noted only the Kindling's activation and the missing ward.

He had not noted the chill. He had not noted the graying.

His quill hovered over the margin of the appendix. The auditor's handbook was explicit: all anomalies in cost transfer must be reported within twenty-four hours. Failure to report constituted a Class Two breach, punishable by suspension of auditing authority and a fine of two thousand crowns. A second breach meant permanent revocation.

He dipped the quill in ink. The nib scratched against the page.

*Addendum, 14 Emberfall. Appendix Section 4, Paragraph 2: Ward efficacy threshold of ninety-seven percent confirmed. Residual three percent transfer to nearest body is absolute. No known mitigation. Implication: proximity to an unwarded Kindling wielder constitutes unavoidable cost absorption for any auditor within range. Recommendation: reassignment of audit personnel or mandatory ward contracting for claimant prior to further field contact.*

The words looked correct, crafted to the handbook's prescribed format. They acknowledged the finding without accusing anyone of concealment. House Vane had disclosed the Kindling and the lack of a ward. They had not disclosed the bleed constant — but then, neither had the Accord's own technical appendix, not in the summary sections auditors were required to master. The information lay buried in the appendix, where only the careful or the desperate would find it.

Kael blew on the ink. It dried black and final.

He should file the addendum and send it to the central archive in High Spire by the morning courier. Request reassignment, or notify House Corrin's ward trade for an urgent contract. Either action would protect him. Either action would fulfill his oath.

His hand did not move toward the dispatch tube.

The silence of his office pressed against his ears. Outside, the vertical city breathed — the creak of rope bridges, the distant call of a hawker in the Lower Market, the steady drip of condensation from the cliff face into the collection cisterns. Thornmere Reach never truly slept. It only grew quieter, the sounds compressing into a hum that vibrated through the stone.

He opened the bottom drawer of his desk. The false bottom came away with a practiced push, revealing a narrow cavity lined with oilcloth. Inside lay a ledger bound in plain gray leather, its pages numbered in his own hand. He had started it the day he received his auditor's seal, three years ago. The first entry had been a test: *Cost-count initiated. Baseline established.*

He turned to the next blank page. The date was already written at the top, ink faded from a previous session. *14 Emberfall, Year 3.*

Below it, he wrote:

*Proximity event logged 11 Emberfall. Kindling activation confirmed. Ward absent. Auditor position: three feet. Subjective cost sensation: cold, peripheral vision loss, duration approx. four seconds. Appendix bleed constant: three percent. Estimated personal cost: unknown. No report filed. First deliberate omission.*

The quill scratched. The words marched across the page, each one a small surrender.

He wrote the cost-count the way his father had taught him, before the Reckoning Accord had stripped the Ashworth name of its lineage-house and reduced it to a title without land. *Count what you spend. Count what you lose. The ledger is the only witness that cannot be bribed.*

His father's voice echoed in the stone-walled room, a ghost summoned by habit. *An auditor who does not count his own costs will eventually count someone else's against them.*

Kael had not understood the warning at sixteen. He understood it now.

He closed the ledger. The oilcloth sealed with a soft whisper. The false bottom clicked back into place. The drawer slid shut.

The dossier remained on the desk. The addendum sat beside it, ink dry and waiting.

He should file it. The courier would leave at dawn. There was still time.

He stood and walked to the window. The glass was thick, warped by the heat of the forge-fires that burned in the Lower Market. Through it, he could see the spine of Thornmere Reach — the great cliff rising into the clouds, the lineage-houses clinging to its face like barnacles on a ship's hull. House Ashworth's former holding was a dark scar three levels below, its windows empty, its banner poles bare. House Vane's aerie was higher, a slender tower of pale stone with a single balcony that caught the last light.

Somewhere on that balcony, or perhaps in the training room behind it, Sol Vane was burning years he would never see returned.

The thought settled in his chest like a stone dropped into deep water. The ripples would take time to surface.

He returned to the desk. His hand found the dispatch tube's lever. The mechanism was well-oiled, designed for speed. A pull, a hiss of compressed air, and the addendum would vanish into the pipes that threaded through the cliff, emerging in the archive's receiving chamber before the morning bell.

His fingers curled around the cold metal.

The open question was not whether he would pull it.

The open question was what he would write in the ledger tomorrow, when the cost came due for tonight's silence.

---

<!-- chapter_date: 16 Emberfall, Year 3 of the Reckoning Accord -->

The ledger fragment smelled of dust and iron gall ink, the particular sharpness of records kept too long in airless spaces. Sol turned the scrap of vellum between her fingers, the edge frayed where someone had torn it from a larger binding. The script was cramped, the hand of a clerk who had written the same columns until his wrist forgot any other shape.

Three percent. She read the figure again. Thirty percent.

The numbers ran in a steady column down the left side: ward contract fees assessed to House Vane, House Mire, House Pell. Each entry marked with the Corrin seal. Each entry inflated by the same precise margin. Not a clerical error. Not a fluctuation. A system.

Her back pressed against the cold stone of the archive alcove, the narrow stairwell that descended from the Ashworth aerie into the shared records vault. The Reckoning auditors were meant to have access. Kael had signed the chit himself, his signature still wet on the parchment she had carried past the guard at the upper gate. She had waited until the bell struck the sixth hour, until the senior clerks had gone to their evening meal, until the only sound in the vault was the slow drip of water through limestone.

She had not expected to find this. She had come for the flight-log of her mother's final Reckoning, the official record of the Kindling burn that had taken thirty-two years in a single morning. The log was sealed. The archivist had shaken his head and cited precedent. But the ledger fragment had been tucked into the back of a bound volume of ward mortality tables, as if someone had hidden it there and never returned.

Or as if someone had wanted it found.

Sol slipped the fragment into her coat pocket, the vellum crackling against the wool. Her fingers were cold. The archive kept its chill year-round, a preservation measure that left her breath visible in the lantern light. She should take it to the Reckoning court. She should file a formal challenge. She should do the thing that followed procedure and waited for justice to grind its slow course.

Instead she climbed the stairwell toward the Ashworth aerie, the fragment burning against her thigh with every step.

---

Kael sat at the long table in the aerie's receiving room, a stack of audit summaries spread before him. The fire had burned low. Shadows pooled in the corners where the lanterns did not reach. He did not look up when the door opened. He did not need to. The tread on the stone was light, deliberate, the gait of someone who had learned to move without announcing herself.

"Ashworth."

"Vane." His voice was flat. He turned a page. "You're late. The vault closes at the seventh bell."

"It's the sixth."

"The vault closes at the seventh. You were there at the sixth. That makes you late by my accounting." He set the summary aside and folded his hands. "What did you find?"

Sol crossed the room. The floorboards creaked under her weight. She stopped at the table's edge, her shadow falling across his papers. She did not sit. She reached into her coat and drew out the fragment, the vellum stiff in her grip.

She slid it across the table.

It caught on the edge of a summary, turned slightly. Kael did not reach for it immediately. His eyes tracked the movement. His jaw tightened. Then he picked it up, turning it to catch the lantern light.

Sol watched his face. The line of his throat as he swallowed. The muscle jumping at his jaw. The way his eyes moved across the column of figures, once, twice, a third time. He did not meet her gaze. He read the fragment the way he read every document: with the thoroughness of a man who had been trained to find the lie in the truth.

"Thirty percent," he said. The words came quiet. "Across how many houses?"

"Every entry I could see. Vane. Mire. Pell. The fragment's torn. There could be more."

"Could be." He set the fragment down. His fingers rested on the edge, not quite touching the numbers. "This is ward fee inflation. Systematic. Decade-long. If proven." He stopped. "If proven."

"The seal is Corrin. The hand is consistent. The margin is exact." Sol's voice pitched low. "That's not accident. That's policy."

"Policy requires proof. A fragment is not a ledger. A fragment is a piece of a story someone tore out." Kael finally looked at her. His eyes were the color of flint in winter light. "Where did you find it?"

"In the mortality tables. Bound into the back of volume three."

"Volume three covers the years eight through twelve of the Accord." He said it like a citation. "The fragment covers years five through fourteen. Someone moved it."

"Or someone put it there."

"Someone with access. Someone who knew the binding order." Kael's thumb brushed the vellum edge. "The archivist is a Corrin appointee. His deputy is Corrin's cousin. The guard at the upper gate answers to Corrin's steward."

"So we do nothing."

"I didn't say that." He stood. The chair scraped stone. "I said a fragment is not a ledger. We need the full record. The master ledger sits in the Corrin strongroom. It never leaves. It never gets copied. The auditors are given summaries. We verify the summaries against the contracts on file. The contracts match the summaries because the summaries are drawn from the contracts. The ledger is the only place the original numbers live."

"And you've never seen it."

"No auditor has seen the master ledger in sixty years. The Corrin monopoly on ward contracts predates the Accord. The Reckoning court accepts their summaries as prima facie evidence. To challenge the summary, you need the ledger. To see the ledger, you need a court order. To get a court order, you need evidence of fraud. The fragment is evidence of nothing except that someone tore a page from a book."

Sol's hands curled at her sides. "So the system is designed to protect the fraud."
