The first flare erupted from her palm, a silent bloom of force that struck the target board she had mounted on the far wall. The board was layered oak backed with lead sheeting. She had scored it with concentric rings, each marked in charcoal.

The impact left a clean dent in the third ring.

Sol did not move. She waited for the burn.

It came as it always did, a slow unspooling of years from somewhere deep in her chest. Not pain exactly. A subtraction. She had learned to count it in heartbeats. Twelve beats for a full year. Seven for half. She had trained herself to feel the boundary between one year and the next, the moment the debt settled into her bones.

This time the count stopped at seven beats and three pauses.

She exhaled and reached for the slate propped against the wall. Chalk dust coated her fingers. She wrote: *Flare 1 — 0.58 years.*

The numbers blurred. She blinked and wrote it again. *0.58.*

She had done thirty flares in the past hour. The first ten had ranged from 0.72 to 0.85. The middle ten had clustered around 0.65. The last five had all fallen between 0.58 and 0.62.

Progress. Measurable. Real.

She picked up the flare-stone again. The crystal had warmed against her palm, drinking the residue of her gift. It would need to cool before the next session. She set it in the iron bowl beside the slate and walked to the water bucket in the corner. The water was freezing. She drank until her stomach cramped.

Her reflection in the bucket's surface showed a face she barely recognized. Cheekbones sharp enough to cut. Eyes that had not slept properly in weeks. The dark circles beneath them were not exhaustion. They were the shadow of what she had already spent.

She was twenty-three. By the old counts she should have had fifty years ahead of her. By the new counts, the ones she kept in the ledger hidden beneath the loose floorboard in her bedroom, she had perhaps twelve. Thirteen if she pushed the technique further. Fourteen if she never used the Kindling again.

But she would use it. The Reckoning was coming. The date had been fixed since the first snow of Year 1. Forty-three days from now. The auditor would stand in the Great Hall of the Spire and read the findings. House Vane would stand or fall on the strength of its claim.

And Sol would be there. She would fly the ancestral route. She would prove the bloodline still held the sky.

She returned to the center of the circle. The flare-stone had cooled to a dull grey. She lifted it, centered her weight, and began the breathing pattern again.

In for four counts. Hold for six. Out for eight. The rhythm was a cage she had built for the fire in her blood. Each cycle tightened the bars.

The second flare caught the second ring. The burn counted to seven beats even.

*Flare 2 — 0.58 years.*

She wrote it down. Her hand did not shake.

The third flare struck the bullseye. The burn was seven beats, one pause.

*Flare 3 — 0.60 years.*

The fourth. Third ring. Seven beats, two pauses.

*Flare 4 — 0.62 years.*

The fifth. Second ring. Six beats, five pauses.

*Flare 5 — 0.54 years.*

She stared at the number. The lowest yet. Her breath came shallow. The chalk snapped between her fingers.

She had not expected to break the six-tenths barrier this soon. The texts she had stolen from the archive — the ones the Corrin ward-traders had not burned — spoke of masters who spent decades reaching 0.5. The Vane line had never produced a master. Her mother had plateaued at 0.83 and stayed there until the day she flew the Reckoning route and never came back.

Sol picked up the broken chalk. She wrote *0.54* again, larger this time. Underlined it twice.

The promise tasted like iron on her tongue. *Not like her. Not like her. Not like her.*

She had been seven when the Reckoning took her mother. Seven and standing in the crowd with her father's hand crushing her shoulder. The auditor had read the findings in a voice that carried no mercy. House Vane, insufficient ward coverage. House Vane, Kindling expenditure exceeding sustainable threshold. House Vane, claim denied.

The ward-traders had descended like vultures. House Corrin had bought the debt for pennies. They had stripped the lineage-house of its flight-rights, its landing platforms, its very name from the registry. Sol's father had drunk himself into the stone floor. Her brother had vanished into the lower reaches, running messages for the smugglers who moved contraband Kindling through the cliff-face tunnels.

Sol had stayed. She had studied. She had stolen the texts. She had built the practice chamber with her own hands.

And now she had 0.54.

She lifted the flare-stone for the sixth time. The crystal flared in response to her intent, hungry and cold.

She breathed. The rhythm held. The fire in her blood pressed against the bars of the cage.

The flare struck the bullseye dead center.

The burn came slower this time. She counted. Eight beats. Nine. Ten. Eleven. Twelve.

Thirteen.

Fourteen.

Fifteen.

She gasped, knees buckling. The slate caught her as she fell. The chalk skittered across the floor.

Fifteen beats. One and a quarter years. A single flare had cost her more than the previous five combined.

She lay on the cold stone, gasping, and waited for the subtraction to settle. It did not feel like dying. It felt like waking up smaller than you were when you closed your eyes.

The ledger in her bedroom would need a new entry. *Session 17. 5 flares at mean 0.58. 1 flare at 1.25. Net expenditure: 4.15 years. Remaining estimate: 8.3 years at current rate.*

She had done the math a thousand times. At 0.6 years per flare, the ancestral route required forty-seven flares. Twenty-eight years. She did not have twenty-eight years.

At 0.54, the route required forty-seven flares. Twenty-five years. Still not enough.

She needed 0.4. She needed 0.3. She needed a miracle she had not earned.

But 0.54 was real. She had held it for five flares. She could hold it again. She would hold it until her fingers bled and her heart stopped and the stone forgot her name.

She pushed herself upright. Her hands trembled as she retrieved the chalk. She wrote: *Flare 6 — 1.25 years. Anomaly. Fatigue factor. Recalibrate.*

She would not call it failure. She would not give the word space in her ledger.

The flare-stone sat in its iron bowl, dull and patient. She could end the session here. She should end the session here. The texts were clear: overtraining the Kindling accelerated the burn exponentially. The masters who pushed past their limits had simply vanished, their bodies consumed by the very gift they sought to master.

But the Reckoning was forty-three days away.

She stood. She lifted the stone. She breathed.

The seventh flare hit the third ring. The burn counted to eight beats.

*Flare 7 — 0.67 years.*

The eighth. Second ring. Seven beats, four pauses.

*Flare 8 — 0.62 years.*

The ninth. Bullseye. Seven beats, one pause.

*Flare 9 — 0.60 years.*

The tenth. Bullseye. Seven beats even.

*Flare 10 — 0.58 years.*

She was back in the rhythm. The anomaly had been fatigue. The technique held. The cage held.

She wrote the numbers. Each decimal a vow. Each entry a wall between her and the fate that had taken her mother.

The chamber door creaked.

Sol froze. The chalk stopped mid-stroke. Her heart hammered against ribs that had already paid too dearly for this night.

Footsteps. Slow. Deliberate. Not her father's shuffling gait. Not her brother's light tread.

A man's stride. Heavy on the heel. Confident in the dark.

She did not reach for a weapon. She had none. She had only the flare-stone in her left hand and the Kindling in her blood and the certain knowledge that whoever stood at the top of the stairs was about to become the nearest soul.

The transfer was involuntary. It always had been. It always would be. No technique could redirect it. No ward contract could shield against it unless the ward was bound and consenting and paid. House Vane had never been able to afford a ward. Her mother had flown the Reckoning route with no one to catch the cost but the empty air.

And now someone was descending the stairs into her secret chamber, and if she flared — if she even breathed wrong — the cost would land on them.

The footsteps stopped at the bottom of the stairs. A silhouette filled the doorway, broad-shouldered and still.

"Vane," said a voice she knew from the Great Hall, from the audit hearings, from the nightmares she had not allowed herself to name. "You're burning the midnight oil."

Kael Ashworth stepped into the circle of lantern-light she had hung from the ceiling hooks. His auditor's coat caught the glow, the silver thread of his house insignia catching fire. His eyes found the target board, the slate, the flare-stone in her hand.

They found the numbers written in chalk.

"Point five eight," he said. "Point six. Point five four."

He did not sound surprised. He sounded like a man reading a ledger he had already memorized.

Sol's throat closed. The Kindling in her blood surged, reactive and wild. She felt the flare-stone pulse against her palm, hungry for release.

She did not breathe. She did not move. She stood in the center of her chalk circle with a year and a quarter already stolen from her tonight and the auditor of House Ashworth standing twelve feet away, the nearest soul in the dark.

"Get out," she said. Her voice cracked. "This is private property. You have no warrant."

"I have the Reckoning Accord," he said. "Article Seven, Section Three. Any Kindling use by a claimant house is subject to audit at any hour. You knew that when you stole the texts. You knew that when you built this chamber."

He took a step closer. Eleven feet.

The flare-stone burned cold in her grip. The Kindling pressed against the cage. She could feel the transfer waiting, coiled and patient. If she flared now, he would pay. 0.6 years. 0.58. Whatever her control allowed.

He would pay because he was nearest. Because the Kindling did not ask permission. Because the system had been built this way on purpose, by the ward-traders who profited from the only sanctioned alternative.

Kael Ashworth stood in her practice chamber and looked at her numbers and did not reach for his seal-stamp. Did not call for witnesses. Did not pronounce judgment.

He looked at her. Really looked. His face was pale in the lantern-light, the sharp bones of it shadowed by exhaustion she recognized. He had not been sleeping either.

"You're going to kill yourself," he said quietly. "Before the Reckoning even begins."

Sol's laugh came out jagged. "That's the plan. Fly the route. Prove the claim. Die with the name restored."

"You won't restore the name if you're ash."

"Then I'll be ash with wings. My mother didn't have wings. She had 0.83 and a corrupted ward contract and a Corrin auditor who pocketed the difference."

The silence stretched. The flare-stone pulsed. The Kindling waited.

Kael's jaw tightened. "My father was not the auditor on your mother's case."

The words hit her harder than any flare. She had researched the records. The auditor's name had been redacted. House Corrin had sealed the file. She had assumed — everyone had assumed — that Valerius Ashworth had signed the denial. He had been the ruling auditor at the time. His seal was on every other Reckoning from that year.

But Kael was saying no.

"You're lying," she said. "The records —"

"Were sealed by Corrin. Not by my father. He recused himself. He wrote a dissent. It was buried."

He took another step. Ten feet.

The transfer hummed in her veins. She could feel it measuring the distance. Calculating the cost. Ready to leap.

"Why are you telling me this?" she whispered. "Now. Here. In the dark."

"Because you're burning years you don't have to chase a number that won't save you. Because I read the dissent my father wrote. Because it says the Corrin ward-contract on your mother's flight was falsified. Because it says the cost-transfer that killed her was engineered."

The words fell into the chamber like stones into a well. Sol felt the bottom drop out of her.

"Engineered," she repeated. The word had no weight. No meaning. It simply existed.

"House Corrin controls the ward trade," Kael said. "They control the contracts. They control the auditors who sign them. Your mother's ward was a Corrin ward. The contract had a clause — a trigger clause. If the Kindling expenditure exceeded a threshold, the cost would transfer to the ward. But the threshold was set below what the route required. She was never meant to survive."

Sol's hand convulsed around the flare-stone. The Kindling surged, a wave of cold fire. She felt the transfer activate, felt it reach for the nearest soul —

She threw the flare-stone into the iron bowl. The crystal shattered. The light died.

The transfer collapsed back into her. The burn caught her in the chest, sudden and vicious. She gasped, doubling over, counting the beats. Eight. Nine. Ten.

Kael moved. Fast. He caught her before she hit the floor, his arms warm and solid around shoulders that had not been held in years.

"Breathe," he said. "Breathe, Vane. Count. I know you know how."

She counted. Eleven. Twelve. Thirteen. Fourteen.

The subtraction settled. She sagged against him, shaking, her face pressed to the silver thread of his coat.

Thirteen beats. One year and a fraction. Gone.

She had just burned another year to stop herself from burning him.

He knew. He had to know. The transfer was involuntary. The nearest soul paid. She had been the nearest soul to her own flare-stone when she destroyed it. The cost had rebounded.

But if she had flared at him — if she had lost control for even a heartbeat — he would have paid.

He was holding her. He was the nearest soul. And he had not moved away.

"Why didn't you step back?" she whispered into his coat. "When you saw the stone. When you knew what it was. Why didn't you run?"

His hand came up, fingers threading through her hair. The touch was terrifyingly gentle.

"Because then I wouldn't be here," he said. "To tell you the truth. To give you the dissent. To make sure you don't fly into a trap they built twenty years ago."

He pulled back enough to meet her eyes. His own were grey in the lantern-light, shadowed and steady.

"The dissent is in my coat pocket," he said. "The original. My father's hand. I've carried it for three weeks. I haven't reported it. I haven't filed it. I haven't told anyone."

"Why?"

"Because the moment I do, House Corrin knows I have it. They'll bury it again. They'll bury me. And you'll fly the Reckoning with nothing but 0.58 and a prayer."

He reached into his coat. She tensed. He withdrew a folded parchment, wax seal intact, the Ashworth sigil pressed into red.

He held it out to her.

"Take it," he said. "Read it. Burn it if you want. But you needed to know. Before the Reckoning. Before you spend the last of yourself on a lie."

Sol stared at the parchment. Her fingers, chalk-dusted and trembling, did not move.

The Kindling in her blood had gone quiet. The transfer slept. The cage held.

But the nearest soul was still standing in her circle. Still holding the proof that everything she had built her life on was a fabrication. Still looking at her like she was something worth saving instead of something already spent.

She reached for the parchment. Her fingers brushed his. The contact sent a jolt through her that had nothing to do with Kindling and everything to do with the fact that he was warm and alive and twelve feet from the target board where her flares had struck true.

The parchment was heavy in her hand. The wax seal unbroken.

"Forty-three days," she said. "The Reckoning is forty-three days away."

"I know."

"And you're giving me this now. Tonight. After seventeen sessions of watching me burn."

"I wasn't watching," he said. "Tonight was the first time I found you. I followed the Kindling residue. It leaks, Vane. Even at 0.58. It leaks into the stone. The Corrin ward-traders have hounds that can track it. If I found you, they will too."

The words iced her blood. "Hounds."

"Kindling-scent hounds. Bred for it. Corrin uses them to find unregistered practitioners. To find wards who break contract. To find claimants who train without license."

He stepped back. The distance grew. Twelve feet. Thirteen. The transfer dormant in her veins relaxed.

"You need to stop," he said. "The training. The flares. Every use paints a brighter target on this house. On you. On anyone who stands near you."

"And if I stop? I fly the route at 0.83. I burn twenty-eight years I don't have. I die in the sky like my mother."

"Then we find another way."

"There is no other way. The Reckoning requires the flight. The flight requires the Kindling. The Kindling requires the cost."

"Then we change the Reckoning."

She laughed again, broken and raw. "You're an auditor. You uphold the Accord. You don't change it."

"I uphold the law," he corrected. "The Accord is law. But the Corrin ward-trade is corruption. My father's dissent proves it. If we surface it — publicly, formally, with witnesses — the Reckoning board has to investigate. The date moves. The terms change. You get time."

"And you lose everything. Your position. Your house. Your father's legacy."

Kael Ashworth stood in the practice chamber of a fallen house, surrounded by the evidence of a girl burning her future one decimal at a time. The lantern-light caught the exhaustion in the lines around his eyes, the tension in his jaw, the white-knuckle grip he kept on his coat where the dissent rested.

"My father's legacy," he said slowly, "is the truth he wrote down and couldn't speak. My position is a chair I sit in while Corrin pulls the strings. My house — "

He stopped. His gaze dropped to the slate, to the numbers she had written in chalk.

*0.58. 0.60. 0.54. 0.58.*

"My house," he finished, "has forgotten what it was built to protect."

He turned toward the stairs. He paused at the bottom, one hand on the rough stone.

"Vane."

She did not move. The parchment burned cold in her hand.

"Keep the flare-stone in the iron bowl when you're not using it. The residue leaks less that way. And — "

He hesitated. The silence stretched.

"And what?"

"And don't flare when I'm in the room. I'd rather not lose another year to your aim."

He climbed the stairs. The footsteps faded. The door creaked shut.

Sol stood alone in the circle. The lanterns guttered. The target board bore the dents of ten flares. The slate held the numbers that proved she could do what no Vane had done in generations.

She held the dissent that proved her mother had been murdered by contract.

She had 8.3 years left by the generous count. 7.3 by the honest one. The Reckoning was forty-three days away.

She walked to the slate. She picked up the chalk. She wrote a new line at the bottom of the ledger.

*Session 17 complete. Net expenditure: 5.15 years. Anomaly: external presence. Transfer averted. Dissent received. Verification required.*

She set the chalk down. She picked up the parchment. She broke the seal.

The handwriting was elegant, precise, the ink faded but legible. *Ruling Auditor Valerius Ashworth, Dissenting Opinion, Reckoning of House Vane, Year -20. The ward contract presented by House Corrin contains a triggered cost-transfer clause set at 0.75 years per flare. The ancestral route requires a minimum sustained expenditure of 0.80. The claimant was mathematically guaranteed to exceed threshold. This constitutes engineered failure. I refuse to sign.*

Below the text, a signature. *Valerius Ashworth, Ruling Auditor.*

And below that, a second signature, added later in different ink. *Sealed by Order of the Corrin Majority. K. Vane, Witness.*

K. Vane. Her father's initial.

He had witnessed the sealing. He had known. He had let them bury it and drank himself into the floor and never told her.

The parchment trembled in her hands. The Kindling in her blood stirred, waking slow and hungry.

She looked at the stairs where Kael Ashworth had disappeared. She looked at the target board. She looked at the numbers that were her only map out of the dark.

Forty-three days. 7.3 years. A dissent that could change everything or get them both killed.

She folded the parchment carefully. She tucked it into the hidden ledger beneath the floorboard. She climbed the stairs to her bedroom, each step a subtraction she could not afford.

The Reckoning was coming. The ward-traders had hounds. The auditor who had held her while she burned a year to save him was the only person who knew she had the proof.

And somewhere in the dark, the nearest soul was waiting for the next flare.

---

<!-- chapter_date: 26 Emberfall, Year 3 of the Reckoning Accord -->

The study smelled of old ink and cold stone, the scent that clung to every room in Ashworth House where decisions were made and buried. Kael stood before his father's desk, his auditor's badge cold against his chest, the weight of three weeks of quiet investigation pressing against his ribs.

Valerius Ashworth did not look up from the ledger. His quill scratched across vellum, each stroke deliberate, measured. The fire behind him threw long shadows across walls lined with precedent volumes, their spines cracked from generations of use.

"You asked for my time, Auditor Ashworth," Valerius said. His voice carried the same flat timbre it had when Kael was a boy requesting permission to fly the lower ledges. "You have it."

"I asked for answers." Kael kept his hands clasped behind his back, the posture drilled into him before he could walk. "The Vane claim file. The annotations you made in the margin of the 312th Reckoning record. The ward assignment that was crossed out and rewritten."

The quill stopped. Ink pooled at the nib.

"Procedure requires that annotations remain internal," Valerius said. "Procedure requires that auditors do not discuss active claimants with family members who hold no standing in the review."

"I hold standing as the assigned auditor."

"You hold standing as my son asking questions in my study." Valerius set the quill down. His fingers, stained black at the tips, folded atop the ledger. "The distinction matters. It has always mattered."

Kael's jaw tightened. Three weeks since the night chase across the lineage-house ledges, since he had caught Sol Vane mid-descent with her Kindling flaring and felt the years burn from him instead of her. Three weeks of waking with phantom heat in his fingertips, of reviewing ward contracts until the text blurred, of tracing the money from House Corrin's trade ledgers to the auditor accounts that approved them.

"The ward who died at the last Reckoning," Kael said. "The Vane ward. Her name was in the file you annotated."

Valerius's expression did not change. A statue carved from the same cliff face as the house itself.

"Mara Vane," Valerius said. "She was not a ward in the contracted sense. She was the claimant's sister. She stood in when the contracted ward withdrew. The record shows she volunteered."

"Volunteered." The word tasted like ash. "She was seventeen. The contract withdrawal happened six hours before the Reckoning. The replacement ward — Corrin-supplied — never arrived. You signed the acceptance of her substitution."

"I signed what the procedure allowed." Valerius's voice hardened. "A claimant without a ward fails. A claimant with a substitute ward, however irregular, proceeds. The law values the claim over the circumstance. You know this. You wrote the brief on it in your third year."

"I wrote the brief on procedural integrity." Kael took a step forward. "Not on looking away when the irregularity bears a Corrin seal."

The fire cracked. A log shifted. The silence stretched until it became a thing with weight.

"Corrin supplies wards to half the Reach," Valerius said. "Their seals are on every contract in this house. To investigate each seal for motive would paralyze the Reckoning. The system functions because auditors trust the paperwork."

"The system functions because auditors do not ask who benefits when the paperwork fails."

"Then you are not an auditor. You are an advocate. And advocates do not sit in this chair."

Kael's breath came slow. The badge at his chest felt heavier. He had come here expecting denial. He had not expected the wall to be built from the very principles he had sworn to uphold.

"The Kindling transfer," Kael said. "The involuntary cost. You knew the mechanism was broader than the disclosed texts. The margin note in the 312th record — 'unquantified bleed radius' — that was yours."

Valerius picked up the quill again. The scratching resumed, a small sound in the large room.

"Speculation in a margin is not evidence in a finding," Valerius said. "You of all people should know the difference."

"I'm not asking for a finding. I'm asking for the truth you wrote down and then buried."

"The truth is that Mara Vane died because the Kindling consumed her years faster than any model predicted. The truth is that her sister, the claimant, failed her Reckoning regardless. The truth is that House Vane fell because it could not afford the ward trade, and no amount of auditor regret changes the outcome."

"Sol Vane is claiming now. The same house. The same Corrin pressure. The same auditor silence."

"Sol Vane is a separate claim. A separate review. A separate Reckoning." Valerius turned a page. "Your assignment is to audit her claim on its merits. Not to retry the past through the present."

"Mara Vane was my age when she died." Kael's voice dropped. "You let her stand in that circle. You signed the paper that put her there."

"I signed the paper that allowed a claim to proceed." Valerius did not look up. "The alternative was certain failure. The alternative was House Vane dissolved, its lineage scattered, its children cast to the wind. You call it complicity. I call it the only choice the procedure allowed."

"A choice you made. A choice you noted in the margin. A choice you never reported."

"Because reporting it would have changed nothing." The quill stopped again. "The ward trade is Corrin's monopoly. The Kindling mechanics are the Crown's secret. The auditors are the hinge between them. We do not break the hinge. We oil it."

"And when the hinge crushes someone?"

"Then we record the crushing. We file the report. We move to the next case." Valerius finally met his son's eyes. The gray irises held no warmth, no surrender. Only the cold clarity of a man who had made his peace with the machine. "You want me to say I failed her. I will not. I followed the procedure. The procedure failed her. That is the distinction that lets me sleep."

Kael felt something fracture inside him, clean and final. Not anger. Not disappointment. Something colder. The recognition that the father he had measured himself against had never been the man he believed.

"Mara Vane's death haunts this house," Kael said. "It haunts the Vane claim. It haunts me. And you speak of procedure."

"Because procedure is all that stands between us and chaos." Valerius rose, the chair scraping stone. He moved to the window, his silhouette sharp against the ember-light spilling across the Reach. "The Reckoning comes in six weeks. Your report on the Vane claim is due in four. I suggest you focus on the living claimant, Auditor Ashworth. The dead have no standing."

Kael did not move. The study door felt miles away.

"Four weeks," he said. "To decide whether I oil the hinge or break it."

Valerius's shoulder tensed, the only tell in a body carved from control.

"Then decide," his father said. "But know this. If you break it, you break your house. You break your position. You break every claim this family has ever protected. The Vane girl included."

The threat landed without force, without volume. It simply existed, a fact of the architecture.

Kael turned. His hand found the door latch. The iron was cold, grounding.

"Good night, Father," he said.

"Good night, Auditor."

The corridor swallowed him. The stone walls closed around footsteps that echoed too loud in the silence. Somewhere below, the Reach breathed — thousands of lives stacked vertical, bound by blood and contract and the slow burn of inherited fire.

He had come for proof. He left with a margin note and a father's refusal.

The question followed him down the spiral stairs, into the cold night air, out onto the ledge where the wind tasted of ash and anticipation.

How many more margins would he have to read before the center finally gave way?

---

<!-- chapter_date: 28 Emberfall, Year 3 of the Reckoning Accord -->

The envelope had no seal. No return address. It sat on Sol's desk beneath the cracked windowpane where the wind always found a way through, plain as a laundry list and twice as dangerous.

She had found it tucked into the gap between the doorframe and the lintel when she returned from the morning's climb. The climb had been routine. The envelope was not.

Inside, a single sheet of vellum folded twice. The handwriting was cramped, deliberate, the ink brown with age or iron gall.

*Contract 7-44. Ward ledger, third folio, line twelve. Consent signature forged. Witness marks altered post-signing. Check the ink bleed on the thumbprint. You have three days before the next audit cycle.*

No signature. No cipher. Just the facts, stripped bare.

Sol read it three times. The wind rattled the window. Somewhere below, the great chains of Thornmere Reach groaned as a heavy gondola passed.

Contract 7-44. The number meant nothing to her. But the implication — a forged consent signature on a ward contract — that meant everything. Wards were the backbone of the Kindling system. Contracted, consenting cost-absorbers. Paid. Controlled. The Corrin-controlled trade existed because consent could be documented, verified, audited. If consent could be forged, then the entire ledger was rotten.

She stood, the chair legs scraping stone. The vellum burned cold against her fingertips.

She needed Kael. She hated that she needed Kael.

---

Kael Ashworth sat in the auditor's alcove on the seventh tier, surrounded by stacks of ledgers that smelled of dust and old iron. The morning light cut pale across the desk at a slant. He had been reviewing the quarterly Kindling expenditure reports for House Merren since dawn. The numbers did not balance. They rarely did.

A knock at the doorframe.

He did not look up. "Enter."

Sol Vane stepped inside. She wore climbing leathers stained grey at the cuffs, her hair pulled back in a rough knot. A smudge of graphite marked her left cheekbone. She carried a folded sheet of vellum pinched between two fingers like something poisonous.

Kael set down his pen. The scratch of nib on paper ceased. In the silence, the Reach's chains sang their endless low note.

"Auditor Ashworth." Her voice was flat. Professional. The mask she wore when she needed something from him.

"Lady Vane." He kept his tone level. "This is irregular. My office hours for private petitions are third bell."

"This isn't a petition." She crossed the room in three strides and laid the vellum on his desk atop the Merren ledgers. "It was left at my door. Anonymous. Read it."

Kael unfolded the sheet. His eyes scanned the lines once, twice. The muscle at his jaw tightened almost imperceptibly.

"Contract 7-44," he said. "Ward ledger, third folio, line twelve."

"I don't know the contract number," Sol said. "But if the signature is forged — "

"Then the ward's consent was manufactured." Kael refolded the vellum with precise creases. "And if one contract bears a forged signature, the integrity of the entire ledger is suspect."

"Corrin's ledger."

"All ward contracts route through Corrin administration. Yes."

Sol's hands curled into fists at her sides. "Can you verify it? Today?"

Kael picked up his pen again. He did not look at her. "I can request the original document from the Corrin archives. The request will be logged. It will be processed in the standard queue."

"How long is the standard queue?"

"Four to six weeks. The Reckoning audit cycle has priority. Every available clerk is pulled to Reckoning preparation."

"The Reckoning is in six weeks."

"Five weeks and three days." Kael made a notation in the Merren ledger. "The backlog is not my invention, Lady Vane. It is a structural reality."

"You're the auditor. You have authority to expedite."

"I have authority to request expedited review. The Corrin archivists control the physical documents. They control the queue." He finally looked up. His eyes were the colour of flint. "I will submit the request this afternoon. I will mark it urgent. Beyond that, the timeline belongs to House Corrin."

Sol stood motionless. The wind chose that moment to shriek around the building's corner, a sound like tearing canvas.

"Five weeks," she said quietly. "The Reckoning happens in five weeks. If this contract is genuine — if the forgery is real — then Corrin's entire ward trade could be exposed before the Reckoning convenes. Every claim they've backed. Every auditor they've influenced. Every house they've crushed."

"Including yours."

"Especially mine."

Kael capped his pen. The click sounded loud in the alcove. "I understand the stakes, Lady Vane. I will do what is within my authority. I will not do what is outside it."

"Even if what's within your authority isn't enough?"

"Especially then." He rose, the chair legs silent on the stone. "The law is not a weapon you swing at convenience. It is a framework. If I break framework to chase a result, I become what I'm sworn to audit."

Sol studied his face. The hard line of his mouth. The tension she had learned to read in the set of his shoulders. She had climbed the Reach's sheer faces since childhood. She knew how to read rock for fracture lines. Kael had fracture lines today.

"Fine," she said. "Submit the request. Mark it urgent. I'll expect the reference number by evening."

"You'll have it."

She turned toward the door. Her hand rested on the frame.

"Sol."

She paused. Did not turn.

"My father's name is not on the Corrin ward ledger. Whatever you think you know about House Ashworth's involvement — "

"I didn't say your father's name."

"You didn't have to." Her voice stayed level. "The tip didn't name him either. That's what makes it interesting."

She left before he could respond.

---

The reference number arrived by courier at the ninth bell. Sol broke the wax seal with her thumbnail. REQ-7744-URG. Urgent review request for Contract 7-44, ward ledger third folio line twelve. Submitted by Auditor K. Ashworth, seventh tier. Status: queued.

Queued. Not expedited. Not prioritized. Queued.

She stared at the slip of paper until the characters blurred. The wind had died. The Reach hung silent, holding its breath.

Three days. The note had said three days before the next audit cycle. Three days until something changed. Or until the window closed.

She climbed to the roof of House Vane's lineage-house that evening. The stone was cold beneath her palms. The city spread below her, a vertical maze of torchlight and shadow, the great chains disappearing into the mist above and the dark below. Somewhere in that maze, a ward contract numbered 7-44 slept in an archive. Somewhere, a signature had been forged. Someone had paid the price.

Her mother's face rose unbidden. The way the Kindling had burned her from inside, years stolen in great gulps because no ward stood ready to absorb the cost. House Vane had been too poor. Too proud. Too late.

Sol had spent six years training to reduce the burn. Six years of secret climbs, of measured Kindling use, of learning to hold the fire in her veins until it smoked instead of flared. She had reduced the cost. She had not eliminated it.

And she had never, not once, controlled who paid the remainder.

The stars wheeled overhead. Cold. Indifferent.

She thought of Kael in his alcove, surrounded by ledgers that never balanced. She thought of the way he had said *I will do what is within my authority* — not *I will find the truth*, not *I will help you*. Authority. Framework. The words of a man who had built walls around himself and called them duty.

But he had submitted the request. He had marked it urgent. He had given her a reference number.

It was not enough. It was something.

She pressed her palm flat against the cold stone of the parapet. The Kindling stirred in her blood, a low hum at the base of her skull. Not called. Not wanted. Just present, as it always was, waiting for the moment when flight became necessary and the cost came due.

Three days.

She would wait. She would watch. And if the archive produced nothing, she would find another way.

She always did.

---

Kael remained in the alcove long after the courier delivered the reference slip. The Merren ledger still did not balance. He had stopped pretending it would.

The anonymous tip troubled him. Not the accusation — accusations were the air auditors breathed. The specificity troubled him. Contract 7-44. Third folio. Line twelve. Ink bleed on the thumbprint. That was not a guess. That was knowledge from inside the archive.

Someone in Corrin administration had sent it. Or someone who had bought access to Corrin administration. The distinction mattered less than the implication: the ward ledger had a leak.

He pulled the Corrin ward index from the shelf. Heavy. Bound in black leather stamped with the Corrin sigil — a coin crossed by a quill. The index listed every active ward contract by number, house, ward name, and consent verification status.

Contract 7-44. House Merren. Ward: Toma Vell. Consent verified: Ninth of Highsummer, Year 2. Witnesses: Corrin Archivist Pell, Notary Heskin.

Kael turned to the third folio. Line twelve.

The entry was clean. Neat script. Thumbprint mark beside the signature. No visible bleed. No alteration marks.

He leaned closer. The lamp light caught the vellum at a slant.

There. A faint halo around the thumbprint. Brown ink feathering into the paper fibres in a way that suggested the print had been pressed onto wet ink — not signed, then printed. Printed, then signed over.

His breath caught.

He had seen this before. Once. In a training case during his auditor apprenticeship. A forger's trick. The thumbprint applied first, the signature drawn over it to create the appearance of simultaneous execution.

But that case had been a minor merchant house. A single contract. This was the Corrin ward ledger. The backbone of the Kindling economy.

If Contract 7-44 was forged, the verification signatures of Archivist Pell and Notary Heskin were either complicit or compromised. And if one contract could be compromised —

Kael closed the index. His hands were steady. They were always steady.

He needed to request the physical contract. The original. Not the index copy. The original would show the ink layers under magnification. It would show whether the witness signatures shared the same ink batch. It would show truth.

He needed to request it through channels. Proper channels. The channels that took four to six weeks.

Unless.

He opened his desk drawer. Beneath the blank request forms lay a different form. Plain vellum. No sigil. A direct appeal to the Chief Auditor — his father — for emergency document seizure under Reckoning Authority Provision Twelve.

He had drafted it three days ago. He had not submitted it.

Provision Twelve allowed an auditor to bypass archive queues when Reckoning integrity was at stake. It required the Chief Auditor's sign-off. It created a record. It created witnesses.

It would put the request on his father's desk. Valerius Ashworth would see the contract number. He would see the ward name. He would ask questions Kael could not answer without exposing Sol.

Kael's fingers brushed the form. The vellum was warm from the drawer's darkness.

*I will do what is within my authority.*

He had told her that. He had meant it.

But authority had limits. And the Reckoning was five weeks away.

He pulled the form out. He picked up his pen. He wrote the date: 28 Emberfall, Year 3.

His hand hovered over the signature line.

The chains groaned below. A gondola passed, its lanterns painting the alcove walls in swinging gold.

Kael signed.

He folded the form. Sealed it with his signet ring. Called for the runner.

"To the Chief Auditor's office. Seventh tier to first. Priority."

The runner nodded and departed.

Kael returned to the Merren ledger. The numbers still did not balance. But the ward index sat open on his desk, page marked, the forged thumbprint waiting under the lamp light like a wound.

He had done what was within his authority. He had used Provision Twelve. He had put his name on a request that would land on his father's desk by morning.

He had not told Sol.

He would not tell Sol. Not yet. Not until he knew what the original contract revealed. Not until he knew whether the leak in Corrin administration was a crack or a flood.

Three days. The tip had said three days.

Kael blew out the lamp. The alcove darkened. The chain-song rose from the deep, steady as a heartbeat.

He would know in three days. One way or another.

---

The ledger sat in the center of Kael's desk, its leather cover cracked along the spine where generations of auditors had pressed their thumbs. He had found it in the third drawer of the archival cabinet in the Hall of Ledgers, tucked behind a stack of expired ward contracts no one had touched in fifteen years. The dust on the drawer's runners had been undisturbed. The dust on the ledger itself had not.

He turned the cover. The first page bore the seal of House Corrin, pressed in wax the color of dried blood. The second page held a signature he knew better than his own.

Valerius Ashworth. Ruling Auditor. Year 1 of the Reckoning Accord.

Kael's fingers went cold. The room around him — the narrow windows looking out over the Reach's sheer drop, the shelves groaning with the weight of every claim and counterclaim since the Accord's signing — seemed to recede. The ledger's pages were crisp, the ink unfaded. Someone had preserved this with intention.

He read the entry again. *House Vane, Reckoning of Year 1. Kindling reserve assessed at 2.3 years. Ward requirement waived by ruling auditor on grounds of documented reserves. Reserves proven falsified by Corrin-supplied assay. Failure recorded. Claim denied. Lineage-house forfeit to Corrin trust pending recompense.*

The handwriting was precise. Auditor's script. His father's hand.

Below the entry, a second notation in a different ink. *Corrin assay fee paid in full. Ward contract slots allocated to Corrin holdings: three. Effective immediately.*

Three ward slots. Three consenting cost-absorbers, contracted and paid, their lives measured out in years to buffer the Kindling of Corrin heirs. The trade that made House Corrin the wealthiest in the Reach. The trade that had starved House Vane of the one thing that might have saved Sol's mother.

Kael's breath came shallow. He had known Corrin pressured the assay. He had known the waiver was irregular. He had not known his father had signed it. He had not known the ward slots had been the payment.

A knock at the door frame. Kael's hand slammed the ledger shut before he thought to move it.

"Enter."

A clerk — young, pale, the sort who still flinched at the sound of an auditor's voice — stepped inside. He carried a folded vellum tube sealed with the Ashworth crest. A hawk striking a serpent.

"From the Lord Auditor's office, Auditor Ashworth. Delivered by hand. The seal is unbroken."

Kael took it. The wax was warm. The ink on the address was still wet.

He dismissed the clerk with a nod. The door clicked shut.

He broke the seal. The vellum unfolded into a single sheet, dense with the formal language of the auditorial compact.

*To Kael Ashworth, Auditor of the Reckoning Accord,*

*By the authority vested in House Ashworth as signatory house and guarantor of the Accord's enforcement, we hereby extend a binding offer of appointment to the position of Ruling Auditor for the Reckoning of House Vane, scheduled for the 14th of Frostfall, Year 3. This appointment carries the full weight of House Ashworth's support and the expectation of a finding consistent with the precedent established in Year 1.*

*The terms are as follows: upon delivery of a verdict denying the Vane claim and confirming Corrin trust administration, House Ashworth shall receive a 12% tithe of all Corrin ward-contract revenues for a period of twenty years, commencing the first day of the new Accord cycle. In addition, House Ashworth shall assume primary audit authority over all Corrin ward assays for the same period.*

*This offer expires at sundown on the 3rd of Frostfall. A written acceptance is required. Failure to accept will be construed as a declination of House Ashworth's patronage and will be noted in the auditorial record.*

*Valerius Ashworth, Lord Auditor of the Reckoning Accord*

Kael read it twice. The date on the offer: today. 29 Emberfall. The ink still wet because it had been written this morning, signed this morning, sent this morning. His father had known he would find the ledger. Or his father had known the ledger existed and had timed the offer to arrive before Kael could act on it.

Either way, the message was clear. Rule against Vane. Confirm the Corrin trust. Take the patronage. Secure the future of House Ashworth.

The price was Sol's house. Sol's legacy. Sol's life, measured in the years she burned every time she summoned the Kindling without a ward to catch the cost.

Kael's jaw ached. He set the offer beside the ledger. Two documents. One proving the rot at the foundation. One demanding he cement it in place.

He thought of the night on the ledges, three weeks past. The wind tearing at their cloaks. Sol's face illuminated by the Kindling's glow, her eyes fixed on something he could not see. The way she had gasped when the cost transferred, the way she had grabbed his wrist as if she could pull it back. *I didn't mean to. I never mean to.*

He had not reported it. He had written *no irregularities observed* in the official log. He had lied to the Accord, to his father, to himself.

Now the proof sat in front of him. His father's signature on the fraud. His father's offer on the cover-up.

Kael stood. The chair scraped against the stone floor. He crossed to the private chest beneath the window — iron-bound, warded against Kindling and mundane fire alike. The key hung on a chain around his neck, hidden beneath his shirt. He had never used it for anything but the auditorial seal.

He unlocked the chest. The interior was lined with velvet, empty save for the seal. He placed the ledger inside. He placed the offer beside it. He locked the lid and turned the key until the mechanism clicked.

The sound was final. A door closing.

He returned to his desk. The surface was bare again. Clean. The light from the windows had shifted while he read, the sun lowering toward the western cliffs. Shadows stretched across the Hall of Ledgers, reaching for the shelves like fingers.

He should send the ledger to the Accord's central archive. He should refuse the offer. He should walk into his father's office and demand an accounting.

He did none of those things.

He sat down. He pulled a fresh log sheet toward him. He dipped his pen. He wrote: *Archival review conducted. No irregularities found in Year 1 Vane proceedings. Corrin assay records consistent with Accord standards. Recommendation: proceed to Reckoning per schedule.*

The lie tasted like ash. He signed it. He dated it. He sealed it with the auditorial stamp.

Then he rose, gathered his cloak, and left the Hall of Ledgers without looking back.

The corridors of the Auditorial Spire were empty at this hour. Most auditors had retired to their lineage-houses or the mess hall. Kael passed the door to his father's office. The nameplate read *Valerius Ashworth, Lord Auditor*. The door was closed. Light seeped beneath it.

Kael did not stop. He descended the spiral staircase to the ground level, his boots silent on the worn stone. The main doors stood open to the terrace, the evening air cool and smelling of pine and the mineral tang of the Reach's cliff face.

He walked out onto the stone. The view spread before him: the vertical city of Thornmere, the lineage-houses clinging to the rock like barnacles, the bridges and lifts connecting them in a web of rope and timber. Somewhere in that web, House Vane hung by a thread. Its claim denied. Its heir training in secret. Its auditor standing on the terrace with a lie in his pocket and a ledger in a locked chest.

Kael leaned against the parapet. The wind tugged at his hair. He watched the last light catch the Kindling-wires strung between the houses, the thin filaments that carried the gift's excess cost to the wards below. They glimmered like spider silk. Beautiful. Lethal.

He thought of Sol's hands, calloused from climbing, gentle when she touched the training dummies in the abandoned aerie. He thought of the way she had looked at him in the archive three days ago — curious, wary, something softer beneath the wariness.

He had not told her. He would not tell her. Not until he decided what the ledger was worth. Not until he knew whether the truth was a weapon or a sentence.

The sun slipped behind the western peaks. The Kindling-wires caught fire with reflected light, each strand burning gold.

Kael watched them until the color faded. Then he turned and walked back into the Spire, the key heavy against his chest, the offer burning in the chest beside the ledger, the log sheet already filed in the official record.

He had until Frostfall to decide. Fourteen days.

The question settled in his ribs like a stone: when the Reckoning came, would he be standing beside her or above her?

---

The wind off the Reach carried salt and the sharp mineral scent of exposed limestone. Sol stood at the edge of the Vane training ledge, her boots braced on the pitted stone, and watched Kael pace the perimeter of the ward-circle he had chalked himself. Three weeks since the hearing in the High Auditor's chamber. Three weeks since he had returned from the Ashworth archive with something folded in his coat pocket that he had not shown her.

He had not mentioned it. He had not mentioned the offer either, the one his uncle's steward had delivered on House Ashworth stationery, dated and sealed. She knew about it only because the steward had misjudged the acoustics of the corridor outside Kael's quarters, and Sol's ears had caught the phrase *rule against the Vane claim* before the door shut.

Kael stopped pacing. He turned, his face pale in the thinning light, and for a moment his eyes held the same flat calculation she had seen in the corridor. Then his expression smoothed into the neutral mask he wore for official business.

"Again," he said. His voice lacked its usual edge. "The cascade sequence. Slow enough that I can track the transfer vector."

Sol did not move. The Kindling stirred in her chest, a low heat behind her sternum that had nothing to do with the cold. She had learned to read the micro-movements of his jaw, the way his left hand drifted toward the inside of his coat where the ledger lived. He had been recording something every night since the archive. She had heard the scratch of his pen through the shared wall of their quarters, steady and relentless.

"Kael." The name came out quieter than she intended. "You've had me run the cascade sequence nine times in four days. My control is not improving because you are not watching. You are waiting."

He did not deny it. The silence stretched, filled only by the cry of a gull riding the updraft along the cliff face. Somewhere below, the lower wards of Thornmere Reach burned their evening fires, the smoke rising in thin ribbons that curled past the lineage-houses like offered prayers.

"I am tracking the data," he said finally. "The transfer efficiency has shifted. Your output is more consistent. The bleed radius has narrowed by point-three meters per flare."

"Because I have been drilling alone before dawn," she said. "While you were in the archive. While you were meeting with your uncle's steward. While you were doing whatever it is you do when you leave this ledge and do not return until the bells ring midnight."

Kael's jaw tightened. The muscle jumped. "The Reckoning is fixed. The date does not move for anyone's convenience. My obligation is to prepare the record. Your obligation is to survive the test. Neither of those obligations requires conversation."

The words were technically true. They were also a wall, built brick by deliberate brick, and she had watched him lay each one since the day he returned from the Ashworth stronghold with ink-stained fingers and a secret he carried like a second heartbeat.

"Then why are you here?" she asked. "Why not send a clerk? Why not file the numbers from your office in the Spire?"

Because you made a promise. The thought rose unbidden, sharp as a blade. Because you stood on this same ledge six weeks ago and let the Kindling burn into your shoulder rather than let me fall. Because the mark on your skin is proof you have already paid a price you never agreed to pay.

Kael's hand pressed flat against his coat, over the place where the ledger rested. Over the place where the transfer mark lay hidden beneath his shirt, a spiral of pale lines that had not existed before the night chase across the Corrin warehouse district.

"Because the Auditor's Office requires direct observation," he said. "Because the Vane claim rests on demonstrated control, and demonstrated control requires a witness who can testify under oath. Because I am the only auditor the High Council will accept for a house with your history."

Each reason was valid. Each reason was a shield. She had spent her life learning to read the spaces between words, the gaps where truth hid behind procedure. The gap yawned wider every day.

"Fine," she said. "The cascade sequence. Again."

She closed her eyes. The Kindling answered the call she had trained into muscle and breath, rising from the deep well of her years like water drawn from a hidden spring. Heat bloomed in her chest, raced down her arms, pooled in her fingertips. The air around her shimmered, the moisture in the wind condensing into tiny crystals that caught the last light.

She released the flare in a controlled spiral, the cascade pattern she had refined through hundreds of solitary dawns. The energy unfurled, a ribbon of golden light that arced toward the target post at the ledge's far edge. The post had been a boundary marker once, before House Vane fell. Now it wore scorch marks in concentric rings, a record of every session.

The flare struck true. The post absorbed the energy with a sound like tearing silk.

Sol gasped. The cost hit her like a fist driven between her ribs, the familiar theft of time. She had learned to measure it in breaths now. This flare had taken perhaps three months. Three months of future mornings, three months of seasons she would not see. A fraction less than last week. A fraction less than the week before.

Progress. Measured in stolen time.

She opened her eyes. Kael stood exactly where he had stood before, his notebook open, his pen moving. He did not look at her. He did not ask if she was well. He recorded the transfer vector, the bleed radius, the output consistency. He recorded the data that would go into the official record, the record that would decide whether House Vane kept its place on the cliff or fell into the sea.

The silence between them was not empty. It was full of all the things he would not say, all the things she could not ask without breaking the fragile truce that let them stand on the same stone.

"Again," he said, not looking up. "The recovery interval is within parameters. We can fit two more before the light fails."

Sol's hands curled into fists at her sides. The Kindling simmered, patient and hungry. She could feel the years draining, a slow leak she had learned to plug with discipline and breath and the sheer bloody-minded refusal to die before her house was restored.

"Two more," she repeated. "Then I drill alone until midnight. As always."

She turned away before he could answer, before she could see if his face held anything besides the mask. The ledge was narrow, bounded on one side by the sheer drop and on the other by the cold wall of the Vane ancestral hall. She walked the length of it, her boots finding the worn grooves where generations of Vane heirs had practiced their forms.

At the far end, she stopped. The wind tugged at her hair, at the hem of her training leathers. Below, the Reach spread out in tiers of light and shadow, the great houses clinging to the rock like barnacles, the bridges spanning the chasms between them. Somewhere in that warren of stone and ambition, House Corrin held the ward contracts that had doomed her mother. Somewhere, her father's name was written in the ledgers of debt that no honest house could ever repay.

She raised her hands. The Kindling rose to meet them, eager and terrible. She shaped the flare, released it, watched it arc into the darkening sky toward no target at all. Just practice. Just waste. Just another few months burned from a life that had never been long enough.

The cost came. She breathed through it, counted the breaths, marked the tally in the mental ledger she kept beside the one Kael wrote in ink.

*Three months. Two months. Four months. One.*

The numbers climbed. The years shrank. She had started this year with perhaps twenty-three years remaining, by the old estimates. She was down to eighteen now, maybe seventeen. Every flare a payment. Every session a small death.

She did not stop until her legs shook and her vision tunneled and the Kindling sputtered like a candle in a draft. Then she slumped against the cold stone, gasping, her palms pressed to the ledge as if she could anchor herself to the rock and refuse to fall.

Above her, the first stars pricked through the violet dark. The Reckoning was coming. The date was fixed. The auditor who held her fate in his pen stood twenty meters away, writing numbers in a book she would never be allowed to read.

She closed her eyes and whispered the only prayer the Vane line had ever known: *Let it be enough. Let me be enough.*

***

Kael Ashworth sat in the narrow window seat of his assigned chamber, the ledger open on his knee, the scratch of his pen the only sound in the room save the wind. The fire had burned low. He had not bothered to feed it. The cold kept him sharp.

The numbers on the page blurred, then resolved. He had been tracking the transfer efficiency since the first session, since the night on the Corrin warehouse roof when the Kindling had flared between them and the cost had split its terrible path across both their bodies. He had expected a pattern. He had found a catastrophe.

*Session 1: Transfer 12% to auditor. Cost to subject: 2.3 years. Cost to auditor: 0.4 years.*
*Session 7: Transfer 18% to auditor. Cost to subject: 1.8 years. Cost to auditor: 0.6 years.*
*Session 14: Transfer 24% to auditor. Cost to subject: 1.4 years. Cost to auditor: 0.9 years.*
*Session 21: Transfer 31% to auditor. Cost to subject: 1.1 years. Cost to auditor: 1.3 years.*

The percentages were climbing. The auditor's share was accelerating. His control improvements were not reducing the total cost. They were redirecting it. Every fraction of a year she saved burned from his own future instead.

He set the pen down. His hand trembled, barely perceptible. He pressed his palm flat against the page, feeling the paper's texture, the faint ridge of dried ink.

The mark on his shoulder ached. It had ached every day since the warehouse, a dull phantom pain that flared when she used the Kindling nearby, that pulsed in rhythm with her heartbeat when he stood close enough to feel the heat of her skin. The healers had no name for it. The Auditor's Office had no category. The ward contracts specified voluntary absorption, negotiated terms, consent signed in blood and witnessed by a Corrin notary.

This was none of those things. This was theft. This was the Kindling's oldest law: proximity demands payment. The nearest vessel pays. No choice. No contract. No escape.

He had known the theory. Every auditor learned it in the first year of training. The Kindling recognizes no authority but distance. But theory was clean and abstract. Theory did not wake him at night with the phantom sensation of years peeling away, of a future shortening in increments he could not measure but could feel in the cold hollow of his chest.

He picked up the pen again. Turned to a fresh page. Began a new column.

*Personal cost-count. 1 Frostveil, Year 3.*
*Baseline projection (pre-transfer): 42 years remaining.*
*Current projection (adjusted for Sessions 1-21): 38.7 years remaining.*
*Accelerated loss rate: 0.16 years per session.*
*Projected loss by Reckoning (22 sessions remaining): 3.5 years.*
*Total projected auditor cost: 4.3 years.*

The numbers stared back at him, black ink on cream paper, indifferent to the man who wrote them. Four years. Four years of his life, transferred without consent, without contract, without a single word spoken between them about what it meant.

He had not reported it. The Auditor's Code required immediate disclosure of any involuntary transfer. The penalty for concealment was censure, loss of license, possible prosecution under the Reckoning Accord. He had committed his first deliberate violation the night of the warehouse. He had committed his second every day since, when he closed the ledger and said nothing to the High Council, nothing to his uncle, nothing to the Corrin ward-masters who would have paid fortunes for this data.

He had committed his third violation when he took the proof from the Ashworth archive and hid it in his coat instead of delivering it to the Vane girl who deserved to know her mother's failure had been engineered.

The paper in his pocket was crisp, folded into a precise rectangle. Valerius Ashworth's signature on the ruling that had stripped House Vane of its ward contract. A Corrin ledger entry showing the bribe payment, dated three days before the ruling. A memorandum in his father's handwriting: *The Vane claim cannot succeed. Ensure the auditor understands the required outcome.*

His father. Valerius Ashworth. The ruling auditor at the time of Sol's mother's Reckoning. The man who had sat in the high seat and signed the order that sent a woman to her death because House Corrin had paid for the result.

Kael's throat tightened. He had known his father was corrupt. He had known the Ashworth name was built on compromises and quiet deals. He had not known the corruption reached this far, or that the deal had cost the life of a woman whose daughter now stood on a ledge burning her own future to save a house that had already been sold.

The offer from his uncle's steward had arrived two days after the archive. *Rule against the Vane claim. The Ashworth seat on the High Council is yours at the next vacancy. Your father's debts to House Corrin will be settled. Your sister's dowry will be secured. All that is required is the ruling.*

He had not refused. He had not accepted. He had said he would consider it, and the steward had left with the satisfied smile of a man who knew the answer was already decided.

Kael's pen hovered over the ledger. The numbers waited. The Reckoning waited. The girl on the ledge waited, drilling alone in the dark, burning years she could not afford to lose while he sat in a warm room recording the mathematics of her sacrifice.

He wrote one more line.

*Decision point: 1 Frostveil. The proof remains concealed. The offer remains open. The transfer continues. The auditor records.*

He closed the ledger. The fire had died. The room was cold, lit only by starlight through the narrow window. Somewhere on the training ledge, a flare arced into the night, golden and brief. He felt the phantom ache in his shoulder answer it, a sympathetic resonance across the distance.

He stood, crossed to the window, and watched the light fade. The Vane ledge was a darker shadow against the cliff face. He could not see her. He could not see the cost written on her face. He could only see the numbers climbing in his ledger, and the proof burning in his pocket, and the offer waiting on his desk like a contract with the devil.

The wind shifted. The smell of salt grew stronger. Somewhere below, a bell began to toll the midnight hour, its voice carried up the rock face on currents of air that had witnessed a thousand Reckonings and would witness a thousand more.

Kael pressed his forehead to the cold glass. His breath fogged the pane. He did not move. He did not go to her. He did not tell her what he knew. He did not refuse the offer. He did not report the transfer.

He waited. The numbers climbed. The years burned. The ledger recorded.

And the girl on the ledge kept burning, alone in the dark, paying a price she had never agreed to pay for a house that had already been betrayed by the man whose signature now rested in his pocket.

---

<!-- chapter_date: 2 Frostveil, Year 3 of the Reckoning Accord -->

The proclamation board in the Grand Hall had been stripped bare overnight. Where the quarterly ward pricing schedule usually hung — laminated vellum, sealed with the Corrin signet, immutable until the next solstice — there was only a single sheet of fresh paper, the ink still tacky when Kael reached it.

He read the numbers twice. Twenty percent. Not a gradual adjustment. Not a phased increase tied to harvest yields or Kindling volatility indexes. A flat twenty percent across every tier, effective immediately, retroactive to any contract not yet paid in full for the current quarter.

His fingers tightened on the rail. The wood groaned.

"Ashworth." The voice came from behind his left shoulder. Magistrate Veyne, his robes stained at the cuffs from last night's ink, did not bother to lower his volume. "You're blocking the corridor. Some of us have houses to notify before their creditors arrive."

Kael turned. The Grand Hall's vaulted ceiling caught the morning light through stained glass, painting the stone floor in shards of red and blue. Around them, the lesser house representatives clustered in knots, voices rising in that particular pitch that preceded shouting. He recognized the livery of House Merrow — three silver fish on blue — and House Tarlan's white stag, and House Veyne's own crossed keys. Three houses. Exactly three.

"Merrow, Tarlan, and Veyne," Kael said. Not a question.

"Coincidence," Veyne said. His smile did not reach his eyes. "The Corrin ledger office reviewed all outstanding contracts last night. These three happened to carry the largest balances. Simple arithmetic."

"Arithmetic doesn't announce itself at dawn with armed guards at the gates."

Veyne's hand drifted to the baton at his belt. "The guards are there to maintain order. The announcement is routine. The Reckoning Accord grants House Corrin full authority to adjust ward pricing in response to market conditions. You know this. You wrote the commentary on Article Seven."

"I wrote that adjustments require thirty days' notice and a public hearing. Not a dawn raid."

"Market conditions changed." Veyne leaned closer. "The Kindling reserves in the Deep Wells have dropped fourteen percent since last quarter. The Cost Wars in the southern reaches have disrupted supply lines. Corrin is protecting the ward supply. If prices don't rise, there simply won't be wards for anyone."

"Then why only three houses?"

"Because only three houses are in arrears." Veyne straightened. "The others paid their quarterly dues on time. Prudent management. Perhaps you should advise your... associates... to follow their example."

The word *associates* landed like a stone. Kael's jaw locked. He thought of the ledger fragment in his desk drawer upstairs — the one he had pulled from the Corrin archive in chapter eleven, the one that showed a pricing algorithm tied not to Kindling reserves but to the debt-to-asset ratios of specific houses. The fragment that proved Corrin had been engineering defaults for years.

It matched this new table perfectly. He had checked before dawn, the numbers burning behind his eyelids as he cross-referenced column by column. The same weighting variables. The same predictive thresholds. The same houses flagged for "restructuring" three years before they missed a payment.

He had not shown it to Sol. He had not reported it to the Auditorium. He had not even burned it, though he should have.

"Twenty percent," Kael said quietly. "On contracts that were already priced at ninety percent of a house's annual surplus. Merrow's ward contract covers four Kindled. Four. At the new rate, their entire surplus goes to Corrin. They'll have to sell land. Or people."

"Wards are volunteers," Veyne said. "Contracted. Compensated. The Accord is very clear on this."

"The Accord also says a ward's family receives the compensation. Not the house. Not the lord. The family." Kael's voice stayed level. "And when the compensation equals three generations of income, volunteering stops being a choice."

Veyne's expression flattened. "You're an auditor, Ashworth. Your job is to verify the legality of the process. Not to moralize about the outcome. The process was followed. The notice was posted. The contracts are binding. If you have evidence of irregularity, file a challenge. But I'd check your own house's books first. The Ashworth ward contracts are up for renewal next quarter."

The threat hung between them, unspoken but crystalline. Kael's father had negotiated those contracts. His father, who had been the ruling auditor when Sol's mother failed her Reckoning. His father, whose signature appeared on the engineered-failure proof Kael had buried in a false-bottom drawer three days ago.

"Good day, Magistrate."

Kael walked away before Veyne could reply. His boots struck the stone with measured force. He needed air. He needed the ledge. He needed to think without the weight of three houses' desperation pressing against his ribs.

---

Sol found her mother's garden door locked. Not unusual — Lady Vane had taken to locking it since the winter storms shattered the greenhouse panes — but the key was missing from its nail by the hearth. She circled the house twice before finding the spare tucked under a loose flagstone in the south courtyard, half-buried in frost-rimed moss.

The garden lay dead beneath the frost. Blackened rose canes clawed at the trellis. The fountain's basin held a sheet of ice thick enough to hold a child's weight. In the center, where her mother had once cultivated night-blooming jasmine for the Reckoning ceremony, a single wooden stake stood driven into the frozen earth. A notice was nailed to it.

Sol pulled it free. Her fingers were numb. The paper crackled.

*House Vane — Notice of Contract Adjustment. Effective 2 Frostveil. Ward retainer increased from 4,200 to 5,040 marks per quarter. Arrears of 800 marks accrued. Failure to remit within fourteen days triggers default clause 4.3.*

Four thousand two hundred marks. Five thousand forty. Eight hundred marks in arrears they did not have, because the quarterly payment had gone to the roof repair after the October storm, and the Kindling suppressants for the winter stores, and the quiet bribe to the Corrin factor who had threatened to audit their flight-gift records.

She read it again. The numbers blurred. Eight hundred marks. Fourteen days.

A shadow fell across the paper. She did not look up.

"Mother."

Lady Vane stood in the doorway of the garden pavilion, wrapped in a shawl that had once been deep burgundy and had faded to the color of dried blood. Her hair, white as the frost on the roses, was braided tight against her skull. She held a cup of tea that steamed in the cold.

"You saw."

"I saw." Sol's voice came out rough. "Eight hundred marks. Fourteen days. Where are we supposed to find eight hundred marks?"

"We don't." Her mother did not sit. "We default."

"Mother. If we default, Corrin seizes the ward contract. They take the house. They take the ledges. They take —"

"I know what they take." The tea cup did not tremble. "I have known for twenty years. Since your father's Reckoning. Since mine."

Sol folded the notice. The creases were sharp. "The other houses. Merrow. Tarlan. Veyne. They got the same notice?"

"Merrow and Tarlan. Veyne paid. Their lord is a magistrate. He knew it was coming." Lady Vane's eyes, pale as winter sky, fixed on Sol's face. "Corrin chooses its targets. Always has. The pricing algorithm —"

"Don't." Sol's hand closed on the paper until the edges bit her palm. "Don't tell me it's an algorithm. Don't tell me it's market forces. I saw the ledger fragment Kael brought back from the archive. The one from chapter eleven. The pricing weights. The predictive thresholds. It matches this table exactly. *Exactly.* They knew. Three years ago, they knew Merrow and Tarlan and us would be in arrears this quarter. They built it into the model."

Her mother was silent for a long moment. The wind shifted, rattling the dead rose canes.

"Then the question is not how we pay," Lady Vane said quietly. "The question is what we do with the time remaining."

"Fourteen days."

"Fourteen days." Her mother turned back toward the house. "Your Reckoning is in eighteen weeks. The Kindling in your veins has not been measured since autumn. You have been training. I have seen the marks on your arms."

Sol's sleeves covered the faint traceries of light — the control exercises, the hours on the ledge learning to hold the burn, to shape it, to *reduce* it. She had not told her mother how much it still cost. How every session left her coughing ash into her palm. How the years slipped away regardless.

"I can hold it," Sol said. "I can reduce the transfer. But I can't choose who receives it. You know that. The mechanic doesn't work that way."

"I know." Lady Vane paused at the pavilion threshold. "But there is a ward contract available. House Corrin listed it this morning. A volunteer. Young. Healthy. Five-year term. The price..." She stopped. "The price is the new rate. Five thousand forty marks per quarter. Twenty years of contract. One hundred thousand marks total."

"One hundred thousand." The number landed in Sol's chest like a stone. "We don't have one hundred thousand marks. We don't have eight hundred."

"No." Her mother's voice was very soft. "But the Merrow girl — the youngest daughter, the one with the cough — her father was in the courtyard an hour ago. Negotiating. He signed a twenty-year ward contract for her. Corrin paid the family forty thousand marks up front. The rest goes to the house debt."

"Forty thousand marks for twenty years of a girl's life." Sol's throat tightened. "She's sixteen. She'll be thirty-six when the contract ends. If she lives that long. Wards don't usually live that long."

"Some do. The ones with strong constitutions. The ones who aren't pushed past threshold." Lady Vane turned fully now, facing her daughter across the dead garden. "The Merrow girl has a brother. Two sisters. Her father looked at the numbers. He looked at his other children. He signed."

Sol stared at the stake in the frozen earth. The notice fluttered in the wind, a white flag on a battlefield she could not see.

"This is the system," she said. "Families selling generations of lifespan to keep their houses standing. Children signing away their futures before they've lived them. And Corrin sits in the middle, setting the prices, holding the contracts, deciding who lives and who defaults."

"It has always been this way."

"Then it needs to change."

Lady Vane smiled. It was a small thing, sad and sharp. "You sound like your father. He said the same thing, the night before his Reckoning. He said the Kindling was a gift, not a currency. He said the wards were people, not inventory. He said the Accord could be rewritten."

"What happened?"

"He burned three years in a single flight. The transfer took his ward — a man named Tomas, forty years old, two children — and gave him eight years of Tomas's life. Tomas died three months later. Your father never flew again." Her mother's eyes held something Sol had never seen before. Something that might have been guilt. "He spent the rest of his life paying Tomas's family. He died in debt to them."

Sol had not known. The stories she had been told — her father's heroic Reckoning, his graceful flight, his tragic early death — had been carefully curated. The truth had been buried in the same drawer as the ledger fragment.

"Kael knows," she said. "He has the proof. He's had it since the archive. He hasn't told me. He hasn't reported it. He hasn't done anything."

Her mother's head tilted. "Kael Ashworth. The auditor. The rival. The man who stands in the Grand Hall while the notices are posted and does nothing."

"He's not doing nothing. He's... he's complicated."

"Complicated is a luxury we cannot afford, Sol. Fourteen days. Eight hundred marks. A Reckoning in eighteen weeks that you cannot pass without a ward." Her mother's hand found the doorframe. "You need to decide what you are willing to burn. And who you are willing to let burn with you."

The door closed. Sol stood alone in the frozen garden, the notice crumpling in her fist.

---

Kael stood on the western ledge of House Ashworth, the city spread below him in tiers of stone and light. The wind came off the Deep Wells, cold and smelling of ozone and old iron. In his coat pocket, the ledger fragment pressed against his hip through the wool.

He had memorized the numbers. The pricing weights. The predictive thresholds. The three houses flagged for restructuring in Year 0, Quarter 3 — Merrow, Tarlan, Vane — all now in arrears in Year 3, Quarter 1, exactly as the model forecast.

The model his father had approved.

He had gone to the archive in chapter eleven looking for precedent. For legal grounds to challenge the Corrin pricing authority. He had found the fragment in a mislabeled box, tucked between tax records and a discontinued trade treaty. The handwriting was his father's. The marginal notes, too. *Adjust weights to accelerate Merrow/Tarlan/Vane default cascade. Ensure ward supply for Corrin contracts. — V.A.*

Valerius Ashworth. Ruling auditor at the time. Kael's father.

The proof had sat in his desk for twelve days. Twelve days since chapter twenty, since the archive, since the offer from his uncle — *rule against Vane, and the Ashworth ward contracts renew at the old rate* — since the knowledge that his father had engineered the failure of Sol's mother, had set the trap that caught three houses and would catch a fourth when Sol's Reckoning came.

He had not shown it to Sol. He had not filed it. He had not burned it.

He had stood in the Grand Hall this morning and watched the notices post, watched the Merrow father sign his daughter's life away, watched Veyne smile, and he had said nothing.

The wind gusted, whipping his coat. Somewhere below, a Kindled launched from a ledge — a flash of wings, a streak of light, the distinctive double-beat of powered flight. The Kindling. The gift. The cost.

He thought of Sol on her ledge, three spires away. He thought of the marks on her arms, the ash in her palm, the years she was learning to hold back. He thought of the involuntary transfer, the mechanic that had no off switch, no target selector, no mercy.
