It goes to the auditor standing witness. It goes to the child in the cradle three doors down. You think you're saving Isolde Vane? You're lighting a match in a powder magazine. The Reckoning is six weeks away. Six weeks. If you surface this now, the hearing collapses. The Accord fractures. Houses Corrin and Ashworth go to open war before the first witness is sworn. And every ward in the Reach pays the price."

"Irrelevant." The word cracked. "They're already paying. The ward in the warehouse. The one who died because I delayed. Because I *waited*."

Valerius went still. "Mara."

"Her name was Mara. She was twenty-three. She had a sister in the lower reach. She had a debt to Corrin that would have taken forty years to clear. She died because the Kindling transfer hit her instead of Isolde Vane, and I knew the mechanism was broken, and I did nothing."

"You did your duty. You reported the incident. You filed the paperwork."

"I filed a lie. I called it a malfunction. I called it an anomaly. I protected the schedule."

"Because the schedule is the only thing keeping this cliff from falling into the lake! You think your conscience is the only weight that matters? You think your guilt is special? Every auditor who has ever worn this badge has carried bodies. The difference is we carry them *quietly*. We carry them so everyone else doesn't have to."

Kael's hand closed on the ledger through his coat. The paper edges bit his fingers. "Mara didn't sign a contract. She wasn't a ward. She was a cleaner. She was in the wrong place because the audit schedule put her there. And I let it happen."

"Then carry her. Carry her the way the rest of us carry ours. But do not burn this house down because you want to feel clean. You want to surface the ledger? Surface it. But know that when you do, I will deny it. I will say you forged it. I will say you were compromised by the Vane girl. I will say whatever I must to protect this house, because this house employs three hundred people, feeds seven hundred, and stands between the Corrin and the total collapse of the ward system. I will end your career. I will strip your badge. I will see you exiled to the lower reaches before I let you hand Corrin a knife at our throats."

The silence stretched. The fire popped. A log shifted.

Kael's hand trembled on the ledger. He felt the tremor travel up his arm, into his shoulder, into his jaw. His father had never threatened him like this. Not explicitly. Not with the full weight of the house behind the words. The auditor's badge at his throat suddenly felt heavy, iron heated in a forge.

"You're asking me to choose," Kael said quietly. "Between the house and the truth."

"I'm telling you the truth *costs*. It always has. The question is whether you're willing to pay, or whether you want everyone else to pay for you."

Kael thought of Mara's sister. He thought of Isolde Vane training on the eastern ledges at dawn, her Kindling flaring controlled and terrible, burning years she couldn't afford. He thought of the ward contracts he had audited, the numbers he had certified, the neat columns that hid the screaming. He thought of the Reckoning six weeks away, the public climax where Isolde would stand alone because her house had no ward, no money, no protection but the training she had scraped together in secret.

He thought of the first time he had stood close to her and felt the Kindling's cost jump the gap, the shock of it, the wrongness, the way his own arm had burned with phantom pain that wasn't his.

He had not reported it. He had not reported the second time. Or the third.

Compromise. Accretion. The slow rot of a man who knew better.

"No," Kael said.

Valerius blinked. "No?"

"I won't give you the ledger. I won't bury it. And I won't let you bury it."

"You'll destroy us."

"I'll destroy the lie. There's a difference."

"There is no difference. Not for you. Not for this house. Not for the Reckoning." Valerius stood. The chair scraped stone. "You have until the hearing. Six weeks. After that, the ledger is evidence. After that, it enters the record whether you want it to or not. But if you surface it now, before the hearing, before the schedule demands it, you choose chaos. You choose war. You choose every ward in the Reach burning for your principles."

"Then I choose chaos."

The words hung between them. Kael's hand still trembled on the ledger. His heart hammered a rhythm he couldn't control. But beneath the fear, beneath the trembling, something else had settled. Something hard and clear.

Mara's face. The warehouse dust in her hair. The way her eyes had gone blank before the healers reached her.

He would not let that be for nothing.

"Get out," Valerius said. His voice was quiet now. Cold. "Get out of my hall. Out of my house. When the hearing comes, you'll stand as an auditor or you won't stand at all. But you will not stand as my son."

Kael didn't move. "I'll stand as an auditor."

"Will you? When the Corrin lawyers tear you apart? When the Ashworth steward testifies that you stole house property? When the Vane girl learns you've had proof of her mother's murder for months and said nothing?"

The words struck. Kael flinched. He had not told Isolde. He had hidden the ledger, hidden the truth, hidden the fact that he had known since the warehouse raid in early winter. He had kissed her on the ledges during the night chase, tasted blood and salt and Kindling-fire, and he had not told her.

The rupture would come. He knew it. He had always known it.

But not today. Not in this hall. Not with his father's threat ringing in his ears.

"Then I'll stand alone," Kael said.

He turned. Walked to the doors. The climb down would be longer. The wind would be colder. The ledger pressed against his ribs, a heartbeat not his own.

Behind him, Valerius said nothing. The fire popped again. The great doors closed on a father who had chosen his house, and a son who had chosen the weight of a dead woman's name.

Kael began the descent. The frost bit his fingers where they clutched the railing. The ledger remained where it was, hidden, waiting, patient.

Six weeks. He had six weeks to decide how to burn.

And the question that had haunted him since the warehouse, since Mara, since the first time Isolde's Kindling had reached for him and taken something he hadn't agreed to give: would the cost be his alone, or would it take everyone he had failed to protect?

---

<!-- chapter_date: 19 Frostveil, Year 3 of the Reckoning Accord -->

The auditor's office smelled of cold ink and older stone. Kael sat behind a desk that had belonged to his father before him, the same desk where Valerius Ashworth had signed the finding that condemned House Vane to the slow bleed of penury. Sol stood in the doorway, her fingers white-knuckled around the rolled ledger she had copied page by page in the Corrin archive's restricted tier. The copy had cost her three nights of sleep and a bribe to a clerk who would not meet her eyes.

She did not sit. The chair remained empty between them, a deliberate gap.

"You knew." Her voice did not shake. It was flat, stripped of the inflection she used when she flew the ledges, when she bargained with merchants, when she lied to her cousins about where the money went. "The Ember Festival. That was the week you found it."

Kael did not look up from the document he was annotating. His quill scratched. The sound was loud in the small room. "I knew there were irregularities in the 31st Reckoning record. I did not know the full scope until the audit team returned from the Corrin vaults."

"The Ember Festival was six weeks ago." Sol unrolled the ledger on the desk's edge, the vellum crackling. "Six weeks. Forty-two days. You sat across from me at the Ashworth feast. You walked the eastern ledge with me when the wind came off the sea. You watched me calculate how many years I had left if I pushed the Kindling to its limit, and you said nothing."

"The investigation was ongoing. Auditor protocol — "

"Protocol." The word cracked in her throat. She swallowed, forced it flat again. "You are the protocol. You are the only auditor with jurisdiction over House Vane's claim. You could have brought the proof to the council the day you found it. You could have told me. You chose silence."

Kael set down the quill. His fingers folded on the desk, controlled, the way she had seen him fold them before a difficult ruling. The way she had once thought meant he was weighing evidence. Now she knew it meant he was weighing consequence.

"I chose to verify before I accused a Great House of engineering a Reckoning failure. Accusations without iron proof destroy the accuser. You know this. Your mother knew this."

"My mother is dead because she had no ward and no proof and no one who would speak for her when the Kindling took its due." Sol leaned forward, both palms on the desk. The ledger's edge cut into her palm. "You had the proof. You had the authority. You had the access to every ledger in the Reach. And you let me believe the failure was ours. You let me believe my mother's wings simply gave out."

"I did not let you believe — "

"You did not correct me. That is the same thing." Her voice cracked then, a clean break in the middle of the sentence. "You chose your investigation over my life. You chose your house's position over the truth that could have saved us. Every day you waited, the Corrin ward contracts tightened. Every day you waited, my cousins sold another ledge-right to keep the house fed. Every day you waited, I burned another year learning to fly with a gift that kills me faster because there is no one to catch the cost."

The air in the office changed. Sol felt it before she saw it — the familiar heat rising behind her ribs, the Kindling waking in response to the spike of rage she could not suppress. She had trained for three years to hold the flame banked. She had learned to count the cost in heartbeats, to measure the burn in increments she could survive. Rage was the one emotion the training could not fully contain.

The ledger's edges curled. The ink on the nearest page darkened, spreading like spilled blood.

Kael's chair scraped back. He stood in a single motion, his hand already reaching for the ward-stone at his belt — a reflex, she realized, not a choice. The stone was inert. He was not her ward. He had never signed the contract. The Corrin trade would never have allowed a Vane to bind an Ashworth.

The heat surged. Sol's vision whited at the edges. She gasped, her knees buckling, the ledger sliding from her fingers to scatter across the floor. The Kindling burned through her — one year, two, three — the count she had learned to read in the flicker behind her eyes. She grabbed the desk's edge, her knuckles white, riding the wave the way she had been taught. Breathe. Count. Contain.

But the rage was a wind she could not tether. The flame found a current and rode it.

Kael cried out — a short, strangled sound. He staggered back, his hand clutching his chest where the transfer mark would bloom, the involuntary brand that had appeared on his skin five times before this. The first time had been in the archive stacks when she had pushed the Kindling to reach a high shelf. The second on the eastern ledge when a gust had nearly thrown her from the stone. The third in the market square when a Corrin enforcer had cornered her. The fourth during the festival disaster that had killed the ward. The fifth three days ago when she had flown the storm wind to prove she could still carry a message.

This was the sixth.

The heat left Sol in a rush, leaving her cold and hollow. She slid to the floor, the stone biting through her trousers. Her breath came in ragged pulls. The ledger pages lay scattered around her like fallen wings.

Kael braced himself against the desk, his face pale, sweat beading at his temples. The new mark would be forming on his chest beneath his shirt — a branching pattern, dark as old ink, each transfer adding another tendril. He had shown her the marks once, in the rare moment between the third and fourth transfers when trust had still felt possible. He had called them the auditor's record. Evidence he was required to report.

He had not reported them.

"You knew," she said again, the words barely audible. "You knew the Corrin trade engineered the failure. You knew your father signed the finding. You knew Ashworth made you an offer to rule against us. And you stood there at the feast and asked me how my training was progressing."

Kael's jaw worked. His hand pressed harder against his chest. "I intended to surface the proof. Before the Reckoning. I was building a case that would hold."

"While I burned. While my house fell. While you let me think the enemy was my own bloodline." Sol's hands curled into fists on her thighs. "You are the auditor. You are the law. And you let the guilty walk because the truth was inconvenient."

"The truth was dangerous. For you. For me. For every house that relies on the Reckoning's integrity." Kael's voice was rough, strained. "If I had moved too early, Corrin would have buried the evidence. They would have destroyed the ledger. They would have made sure the Reckoning proceeded on schedule with a finding against Vane. The only way to win was to wait until the proof was unassailable."

"And in the meantime?" Sol's laugh was a broken thing. "In the meantime I trusted you. I told you things I have never told anyone. I let you see the training logs. I let you stand close enough to take the burn. Every time the Kindling flared near you, you absorbed years I cannot afford to lose. And you never told me why it kept happening."

"I could not tell you without telling you everything. The two are bound together."

"Then you should have told me everything. The day you found the ledger. The day you realized what it meant." She pushed herself up, her legs unsteady. The cold in her bones was deeper than the Kindling's aftermath. It was the cold of realizing that every moment of connection between them had been built on a lie of omission. "You chose your case over my life. That is the truth of it. All the rest is justification."

Kael did not argue. He did not reach for her. He stood braced against his father's desk, the new mark burning beneath his shirt, the old marks fading but never gone, and he let her see the weight of it on his face. The first time she had seen him without the auditor's mask.

It was worse than the mask.

Sol gathered the scattered pages with trembling fingers. The vellum was warm where her skin had touched it, the ink still darkened in the spots where the Kindling had licked the edges. She rolled the ledger carefully, precisely, the way she had been taught to roll a flight-chart before a storm run.

"I am going to the council," she said. "Today. With this. With the witness statements I copied from the archive. With the ward contract ledgers that show Corrin's pricing structure for the houses that failed." Her voice steadied, hardened. "I am going to tell them that the auditor assigned to my house's claim knew the truth for six weeks and reported nothing."

Kael went still. "If you do that, the Reckoning will be delayed. The council will investigate the auditor. Ashworth will be censured. My father's record will be reopened. You will destroy the only house willing to stand against Corrin."

"Ashworth stood against Corrin by offering you a position to rule against me. That is not standing. That is surrender with a better price." Sol tied the ledger's cord. Her hands did not shake anymore. "You made your choice. Now I make mine."

She turned for the door. The corridor beyond was dim, the gas-lamps burning low against the Frostveil gloom. The ledges outside would be slick with ice. The wind would be biting. She would fly to the council hall because walking would take too long and she had no intention of giving Corrin's agents time to intercept her.

"Sol."

She paused at the threshold. Did not turn.

"The transfer." Kael's voice was quiet. "It took three years this time. I counted."

She did not answer. The number settled in her chest alongside the cold. Three years. Three years stolen from the future she had been calculating, three years added to the debt she could never repay. The Kindling did not care about justice. It did not care about betrayal. It burned, and the cost found the nearest body, and the nearest body had been him.

Again.

She stepped into the corridor. The door swung shut behind her, the latch catching with a final click.

The ledges waited. The wind waited. The council hall waited at the top of the Reach, its spires dark against the winter sky.

She spread her wings and launched herself into the cold, the Kindling waking behind her ribs like a second heartbeat, and wondered how many years she had left to burn before the truth she carried consumed them both.

---

<!-- chapter_date: 20 Frostveil, Year 3 of the Reckoning Accord -->

The ledge outside Kael's chambers caught the morning light at a slant that turned the frost into something like crushed glass. He had not slept. The documents spread across the stone table were the same ones he had memorized weeks ago, the same ones he had carried in a locked case beneath his formal robes every day since the hearing in the Hall of Ledgers. Valerius Ashworth's signature on the Corrin ledger. The transfer dates. The ward contract amendments backdated to the week before Sol's mother stood for her Reckoning.

Kael's fingers traced the ink lines without seeing them. His mind kept returning to the calculation he had built in the dark hours before dawn. Nine days until the Reckoning. Seven working days before the final submission deadline for evidence. Three days to prepare a formal challenge that would survive Corrin's objections. One day to deliver it to the Tribune's office before the seals closed.

The numbers had seemed manageable when he first sketched them. They had seemed like a path.

Then Sol had stood in his doorway at midnight, her face pale in the lantern light, holding the copied ledger pages he had never sent her. The pages he had hidden in a false bottom of his travel case. The pages he had decided to release on his own timeline, after he had secured his father's testimony, after he had negotiated the terms of his own survival.

She had not shouted. She had asked one question. When did you know.

He had told her. All of it. The hearing date. The offer from his uncle. The night he burned the original documents in the fireplace of his childhood room because keeping them was treason and copying them was worse.

Now the morning found him alone with the arithmetic of his choices.

Footsteps on the stone stairs. Heavy, deliberate. Not a servant. Not a messenger.

Kael did not turn. The door to his chambers opened behind him. He heard the intake of breath, the pause, the rustle of wool against wool as Sol crossed the threshold.

"You are still here," she said. Her voice was flat. Not cold. Flat, as though something had been scraped out of her during the night.

"I have not left." Kael kept his eyes on the ledger. "I was reviewing the submission timeline."

"Nine days." Sol stopped two paces behind him. He felt the heat of her before he saw her shadow stretch across the table. "You said nine days. You have known for months."

"Since the hearing in the Hall of Ledgers. Twenty Frostveil last year." Kael turned the page. The ink did not blur. "The offer from House Ashworth came three days later. My uncle's terms were explicit. Rule against Vane and the ward contract portfolio transfers to Ashworth management. Corrin retains the trade routes. Ashworth gains the enforcement authority."

"And you calculated." Sol's voice hardened. "You calculated how long you could wait. How much you could trade. Whether your father's testimony would hold more weight if you delivered it yourself or if you let the Tribune's office find it."

"I calculated how to win." Kael stood. The stool scraped stone. He faced her. "Not how to survive. How to win. The difference matters."

"Does it." Sol's eyes were the color of the sky before snow. "Because from where I stand the calculation looks like this. You knew Corrin engineered my mother's failure. You knew your father signed the order. You knew House Ashworth was offered the spoils. And you decided the best use of that knowledge was to hold it until you could negotiate your own position."

"I decided the best use was to build a case that cannot be dismantled." Kael's hands curled at his sides. "A single ledger page is not proof. A copied signature is not testimony. Valerius Ashworth will deny the signature. Corrin will claim forgery. The Tribune's office will demand chain of custody, witness corroboration, magical verification of the ink. If I present this tomorrow they will bury it in procedure and the Reckoning will proceed on schedule with House Vane condemned."

"Then present it today." Sol took a step forward. The ledge creaked under her boot. "Present it now. Walk into the Tribune's office and lay it on the desk. Let them bury it. Let them try. At least the record will show the evidence existed before the verdict."

"And when they dismiss it as a Vane fabrication? When Corrin produces witnesses who swear the documents were planted? When my father testifies that I stole family records to protect a fallen house?" Kael's voice rose. "The Reckoning is not a trial. It is a political execution dressed in legal robes. The only way to stop it is to make the cost of proceeding higher than the cost of stopping. That requires leverage. Leverage requires time."

"Time." Sol laughed. The sound had no humor in it. "You speak of time as though it belongs to you. As though the nine days until the Reckoning are yours to spend. As though every hour you spend calculating is not an hour my house burns."

"Your house is not burning." The words left Kael's mouth before he could stop them. "Your house stands. Your claim is intact. The Reckoning has not happened."

"Because I have been holding the line alone." Sol's hands clenched at her sides. "Because I have been training in secret for three years while you sat in your auditor's chair and watched. Because every time I use the Kindling the cost lands on someone near me and I have no ward, no contract, no one who chose to absorb it. And you knew. You knew the mechanic was worse than disclosed. You knew the transfer was involuntary. You knew in chapter ten and you did not report it. You compromised then. You have been compromising ever since."

The air between them grew thin. Kael felt the familiar pressure behind his ribs, the phantom ache in his forearm where the first transfer had marked him. He had not told her about the marks. He had not told her about the nights he woke gasping with years he had not lived.

"I did not report it because reporting it would have triggered an immediate audit of House Vane." Kael's voice dropped. "An audit you would not have survived. Your training was not complete. Your control was not sufficient. The auditor's finding would have been incompetence, not corruption. The Reckoning would have been called early. You would have stood before the Tribunal with no preparation, no evidence, no leverage. You would have lost."

"So you protected me by lying to me." Sol's chin lifted. "You protected me by hiding the proof that could have freed us both. You protected me by letting me believe the system might work if I just prepared hard enough."

"I protected you by giving you time to become strong enough to survive what comes next." Kael met her gaze. "The Kindling transfer is involuntary. You cannot choose who pays. But you can choose how much burns. That control takes years. You have had three. In three more you might have been ready. In nine days you will stand in the Great Hall and the Kindling will rise whether you are ready or not. I needed you to have those three years. I needed the Reckoning to arrive on my timeline, not Corrin's."

"Your timeline." Sol repeated the words like a curse. "Not mine. Not my mother's. Not the ward who died in the Hall of Ledgers because you delayed surfacing the proof. You told me about the delay. You told me about the ward. You did not tell me the ward died because Corrin moved the hearing date after you hesitated."

The ward. The public event in chapters twenty-six through twenty-eight. The cost that had landed on the page. Kael had read the report. He had seen the name. He had calculated the probability that the ward's death would force Corrin's hand, that the public outcry would create an opening for the evidence.

The calculation had been wrong. The ward had died. Corrin had tightened their grip. The opening had closed.

"That was not my calculation." Kael's throat tightened. "That was Corrin's response. I did not choose the hearing date. I did not choose the ward. I did not choose the transfer that killed her."

"You chose to wait." Sol's voice cracked. "You chose to wait because you believed your timeline was more important than her life. You chose to wait because you believed you could control the outcome. You cannot control the outcome. No one can. The Kindling proves that. The transfer proves that. The only thing you control is whether you tell the truth when it matters."

"I am telling the truth now." Kael reached for her. His hand stopped inches from her sleeve. "I am telling you that I have a plan. A real plan. Not hope. Not prayer. A plan that requires the next seven days. If you let me execute it, the Reckoning will not happen on Corrin's terms. It will happen on ours."

"And if it fails?" Sol did not step back. She did not lean into his reach. "If your plan fails, if Corrin buries the evidence, if your father lies, if the Tribune's office delays until the Reckoning passes? What then?"

"Then I will stand beside you in the Great Hall." Kael's palm pressed flat against the stone table. "And I will use my authority as auditor to enter a dissenting opinion on the record. A formal objection. It will not stop the verdict. It will not save your house. But it will create a legal fracture that can be exploited in the appeals court. It will buy you years."

"Years." Sol's eyes searched his face. "Years of what? Running? Hiding? Watching everyone near me burn while I learn to control a gift that was designed to kill me? Years of knowing you had the proof and chose your timeline over my mother's life?"

"Years of being alive." Kael's voice broke. "That is what I am offering. Life. Not justice. Not vindication. Life. The system does not give justice. It gives outcomes. I am trying to give you the outcome where you survive."

Sol was silent for a long moment. The wind shifted along the cliff face, carrying the scent of pine and cold iron. Somewhere below, a bell tolled the midmorning hour.

"Nine days," she said finally. "You say you have a plan for seven. That leaves two days of margin. Two days for everything to go wrong. Two days for Corrin to produce a witness you did not anticipate. Two days for your father to refuse the stand. Two days for the Tribune's clerk to lose the filing."

"Two days is standard margin." Kael's mind was already running the contingencies. "I have accounted for —"

"Accounted for." Sol's laugh was a sharp edge. "You account for variables like they are numbers on a page. You do not account for the ward who dies because the hearing runs long. You do not account for the child in the gallery who inhales the transfer because she stood too close. You do not account for the fact that every hour you spend calculating is an hour you are not acting."

"I am acting." Kael's patience frayed. "I have been acting for months. I have been building the case, securing the testimony, mapping the Corrin financial network. I have been doing the work that makes the evidence admissible. You want me to walk into the Tribune's office today with a copied ledger and a hope. That is not action. That is suicide."

"And waiting is not?" Sol's hand rose, palm outward. "Stop. I do not want your calculations. I do not want your timeline. I want the proof in the Tribune's office before the sun sets. I want the record to show that House Corrin engineered a failure and House Ashworth covered it up. I want the world to know before the Reckoning begins."

"Then you do not understand how the world works." Kael's frustration surged. "The world does not care what the record shows. The world cares who holds the seals. Corrin holds the seals. Ashworth holds the enforcement. The Tribune holds the procedure. You cannot beat them by showing them the truth. You beat them by making the lie too expensive to maintain."

"And how do you make the lie expensive?" Sol's voice dropped to a whisper. "By sacrificing more wards? By letting more people burn while you calculate the optimal moment? By standing beside me in the Great Hall and letting the Kindling take you too?"

The words hung between them. Kael felt the marks on his forearm pulse, a phantom heat that had no source and no end. He had not told her about the marks. He had not told her that every transfer had carved years from his life since the first forced proximity in the training hall. He had not told her that he had already paid a price he could not reclaim.

"I would not let it take me," he said quietly. "I have wards. Contracted. Consenting. Paid."

"Wards from the Corrin trade." Sol's fingers curled. "Wards who have no choice but to sell their years because the system you serve makes them currency. Wards who absorb the cost you should carry. That is your solution. That is your plan."

"It is the only solution that exists within the law." Kael's jaw tightened. "Unless you have a better one. Unless you can rewrite the Kindling mechanic. Unless you can make the transfer voluntary. Unless you can create a ward system that does not require poverty and desperation."

Sol was silent. Her breath fogged in the cold air. The ledge stretched behind her, narrow and dangerous, dropping away into the white void of the Reach.

"I do not have a better solution," she said at last. "But I know that yours requires me to trust you. And trust requires truth. Not calculations. Not timelines. Truth. You knew. You hid it. You chose your timeline over my right to know. That is the truth."

"I chose your survival over your right to know." Kael did not look away. "I would make the same choice again. Every time. Because the alternative is you standing in the Great Hall unprepared, the Kindling rising, the transfer hitting whoever stands nearest, and you watching them burn because you did not have the control to stop it. That is the alternative. That is what I calculated. That is what I prevented."

"Did you prevent it?" Sol's eyes were wet. "Or did you just delay it? The Reckoning is in nine days. The Kindling will rise. The transfer will hit whoever stands nearest. You have not changed that. You have only calculated how to make the cost acceptable."

"Acceptable is not the word." Kael's voice was raw. "Survivable. I am trying to make it survivable."

"For whom?" Sol turned. The movement was sharp, decisive. Her boots scraped the stone as she faced the ledge's outer edge. "For you? For your house? For the system you keep serving even while you try to break it?"

"For you." The word cracked in the wind. "For you. Always for you."

Sol did not turn back. She walked to the ledge's boundary, where the stone ended and the air began. The cliff face fell away beneath her, thousands of feet of vertical drop to the mist-shrouded valley floor. The lineage houses clung to the rock like barnacles, connected by bridges and stairways and narrow paths that had claimed more lives than the Kindling.

Kael watched her. He wanted to follow. He wanted to grab her arm and force her to listen to the rest of the plan, the contingencies, the witness list, the financial trail that led straight to Corrin's vault. He wanted to make her understand that he had not chosen this, that every compromise had been a surrender to a reality he hated, that he had burned pieces of himself to buy her time.

He stayed at the table. The documents waited. The timeline waited. The nine days ticked down.

"Nine days," Sol said to the void. Her voice carried on the wind. "Nine days and the Reckoning begins. Nine days and I stand in the Great Hall. Nine days and the Kindling rises. You have seven days for your plan. That leaves two days for me to prepare for the moment your plan fails."

"It will not fail." Kael's hand found the ledger's edge. "I have accounted for every variable."

"You accounted for the ward," Sol said. "You accounted for the hearing date. You accounted for your father's testimony. You did not account for me finding out. You did not account for the possibility that I would not wait for your timeline."

She took another step. Her heels hung over nothing.

"Sol." Kael moved. The stool fell. His boots struck stone. "Step back. Please."

She did not move. The wind tugged at her hair, at the hem of her coat. The cliff face was a wall of ice and shadow. The other houses were distant lights, silent witnesses.

"I am not waiting," she said. "I am not calculating. I am going to the Tribune's office. Today. Now. I am filing the evidence myself. If Corrin buries it, the world will know they buried it. If Ashworth suppresses it, the world will know they suppressed it. If the Reckoning proceeds on corrupted grounds, the world will know the grounds were corrupted. That is my plan. That is my timeline. That is the only truth I can offer."

"Sol." Kael reached the ledge's edge. He stopped one pace behind her. The distance between them was measured in heartbeats, in the width of a hand, in the space where the transfer lived. "If you file today, Corrin will move the Reckoning forward. They will claim emergency procedure. They will condemn House Vane before the week ends. You will stand in the Great Hall with no preparation, no control, no wards. The Kindling will rise. The transfer will hit whoever stands nearest. You will watch them burn."

"Then I will stand alone." Sol's shoulders squared. "I will stand alone and I will control what I can. I will make the cost as small as possible. I will not let anyone else pay for my survival."

"You cannot control who pays." Kael's voice was desperate now. "That is the mechanic. That is the horror. The transfer is involuntary. It hits the nearest person. If you stand alone, it hits the nearest person. If you stand beside me, it hits me. I have wards. I have contracts. I can survive it. You cannot ask me to let you face it alone."

"I am not asking." Sol turned. Her face was streaked with wind and something else. "I am telling. Nine days. Seven for your plan. Two for mine. If your plan works, I will withdraw my filing. If it does not, the record exists. The truth exists. That is the only leverage that matters."

She walked past him. The ledge was narrow. Her shoulder brushed his. The contact sent a jolt through him, the familiar phantom heat, the ghost of transfers past and future.

He did not stop her. He did not grab her. He watched her descend the stairs, her figure growing smaller against the cliff face, moving toward the Tribune's office, toward the truth, toward the Reckoning that waited in nine days.

The documents on the table caught the light. Valerius Ashworth's signature. The transfer dates. The proof he had hidden, the proof he had calculated, the proof he had believed he could control.

He picked up the pen. The ink flowed. The ledger waited.

Nine days. Seven days. Two days.

The clock was moving. The calculation had changed. The variables had shifted. The plan was broken.

He began to write.

---

<!-- chapter_date: 22 Frostveil, Year 3 of the Reckoning Accord -->

The door of the Vane spire did not slam. It swung wide on hinges that had not been oiled in two winters, and the cold rushed in like a living thing, biting at the hem of Sol's coat, at the raw skin of her wrists where the Kindling marks pulsed faint and old. She did not look back. She had practiced this walk in the dark of her room, boots on stone, breath measured, the weight of her decision settled in her chest like a stone in a sling. The corridor stretched ahead, narrow and lit by the failing glow of witch-lamps. Portraits of Vane ancestors watched from the walls, their eyes painted with the same fierce gold that burned in her own blood. Some had died in the Reckoning. Some had died before it, consumed by the gift they carried. Her mother's portrait hung at the corridor's end, framed in black iron, the gold leaf on the frame peeling at the corners. Sol passed it without slowing. The air grew colder with every step. The spire's outer door loomed, banded iron and oak, the Vane sigil carved deep into the wood. A thorned rose wrapped around a falling star. She pushed it open. The wind caught her immediately, tearing at her hair, at the edges of her coat. The Reach spread below, a vertical city carved into the cliff face, lineage-houses stacked like cards in a dealer's hand, connected by bridges of rope and iron, by stairways cut into the rock itself. The Kindling in her veins answered the height, the drop, the ancient call to flight. She breathed through it, the way she had taught herself, counting the beats of her heart, measuring the cost before it could take her. Seven years since her mother's Reckoning. Four since she had first felt the Kindling wake in her own bones. Two since Kael Ashworth had walked into the spire with his auditor's badge and his neutral's mantle and the lie he carried like a second skin. The wind screamed through the stone teeth of the Reach. Sol stood on the threshold, one boot on the threshold, one on the empty air beyond, and said the words she had come to say. "I will face the Reckoning alone," she said to the wind, to the cliff, to the empty corridor behind her. "No ward. No auditor's mercy. No house's protection. Mine." The declaration hung in the air, stolen by the gusts before it could settle. She stepped off the threshold. The Kindling caught her, not as a bird catches air but as fire catches dry grass, sudden and hungry and bright. Gold light flared around her, visible against the grey sky, and she rose. Not flew. Rose. The distinction mattered. Flight implied control. This was surrender to a force older than her bloodline, a force that burned time for lift. She felt the years peel away, thin as onion skin, each second of ascent costing her a day, a week, a month. She had trained to reduce the cost. She had trained until her fingers bled and her vision tunneled and the witch-lamps in her room shattered from the pressure of her will. She could not stop the burning. She could only make the flame burn cleaner. The spire fell away beneath her. The Reach opened up, houses clinging to the cliff like barnacles, the great chain-lift creaking in its groove, the market squares tucked into natural caves, the ward-houses of Corrin gleaming with stolen gold. She climbed higher, the air thinning, the cold sharpening to a blade's edge. Her hands clenched at her sides. The Kindling marks on her wrists glowed through her gloves, pulsing in time with her heart. Each beat cost her. Each breath cost her. She rose until the spire was a needle below, until the Reach was a map spread at her feet, until the wind carried nothing but its own voice. Then she turned north, toward the Reckoning grounds, toward the stone circle where the auditors would sit in judgment, where the Kindling would be measured and weighed and found wanting or sufficient. She flew alone. The way she had always known she would.

***

Kael stood in the corridor where she had left him. The door swung slowly shut behind her, the iron band catching on the frame, the thud echoing in stone that remembered every footfall of every Vane who had ever lived. The witch-lamps guttered. The portraits watched. He did not move. His fingers closed around the ledger in his coat pocket, the leather worn smooth at the corners, the clasp cold against his palm through the wool. The weight of it pulled at his shoulder, a physical thing, heavier than its pages and binding had any right to be. He had carried it for forty-three days. Since the night in the archives when the dust had thickened on the shelves and the candle had burned low and he had found the entry in Valerius Ashworth's hand, precise and damning. *House Vane claim denied. Corrin ward-contract ratified. Cost transfer validated per Section 7.* The ink had not faded. The seal had not cracked. The date was fifteen years past, the week of Sol's mother's Reckoning. Kael's father had signed it. Valerius Ashworth, Chief Auditor of the Reckoning, had signed the order that condemned a woman to death by Kindling because House Corrin had bribed the ward-trade and House Vane had been too poor to buy a buffer. The proof had sat in the archives for fifteen years. Kael had held it for forty-three days. He had not sent it to Sol. He had not burned it. He had not delivered it to the Reckoning board. He had carried it in his coat like a heart he had cut from his own chest, beating still, accusing him with every pulse. The corridor smelled of cold stone and old dust and the faint metallic tang of Kindling residue, the scent that clung to every Vane surface, every Vane body. Sol's scent. He had memorized it without meaning to. The way she smelled of wind and iron and something sweeter underneath, something like crushed mint or winter berries. The way her fingers trembled when she thought no one was watching. The way she counted years on her fingers in the dark, a private arithmetic of loss. He knew her count. He had watched her do it. Seven years since her mother. Four since her own waking. Two since him. He had his own count. Ten years since his first audit. Three since his father's death. Forty-three days since the ledger. Two hours since she had looked at him with eyes that held nothing but betrayal and said *you knew* and he had not denied it. The silence of the spire pressed against his ears. He should leave. His role required him at the Reckoning grounds by midweek. The journey from the Reach to the stone circle took three days by chain-lift and foot, longer if the weather turned. He had preparations to make. Reports to file. The neutral auditor's mantle did not wear itself. His feet did not move. The portraits watched. His father's face was not among them. Valerius Ashworth had never been Vane. But his signature was on the ledger page, and that made him part of this house's history whether the portraits acknowledged it or not. Kael withdrew the ledger from his coat. The leather creaked. The clasp resisted, then yielded with a soft click. He opened it to the marked page. The entry stared back, unyielding. *House Vane claim denied. Corrin ward-contract ratified. Cost transfer validated per Section 7.* Valerius Ashworth's hand. Cramped, precise, the ink pressed deep into the vellum as if the pen had been driven by something harder than duty. Below it, a second entry in a different hand, later, smaller. *Audit review confirmed. No irregularities found. K. Ashworth.* His own hand. His own signature. Dated three weeks after he had found the first entry. He had signed the review. He had certified the lie. The ink had not faded. The seal had not cracked. The weight in his coat was not the ledger. It was the forty-three days. The two hours. The seven years and four years and two years he had watched her count on her fingers in the dark, and the ten years and three years he had counted on his own, and the years the Kindling had taken from her mother and would take from her and had already taken from him in ways no ledger recorded. The Kindling marks on his own wrists, hidden beneath his auditor's cuffs, pulsed once. A phantom rhythm. He had never flown. He had never been forced to use the gift. But the cost transfer was involuntary, the law absolute, and he had stood close enough, often enough, that the burns on his skin told a story his reports could not. The marks were evidence. He was required to report them. He had not. The first compromise. The forty-third day. The second hour. Kael closed the ledger. The clasp clicked. He returned it to his coat pocket, the leather warm now from his hand, the weight settling against his ribs. The corridor was silent. The witch-lamps had died. The portraits watched from darkness. He did not move. The Reckoning was eighteen days away. Eighteen days to decide what kind of auditor he would be. What kind of man. What kind of son. The wind howled outside the spire, shaking the stone teeth of the Reach. Somewhere north, a gold light cut through the grey sky, rising toward the stone circle. He did not watch it go. He stood in the corridor of a fallen house, the ledger heavy against his heart, and waited for the dark to give him an answer he already knew he would not like.

---

<!-- chapter_date: 23 Frostveil, Year 3 of the Reckoning Accord -->

The council chamber smelled of old vellum and the sharp mineral tang of the cliff face bleeding through the stone. Kael stood at the center of the semicircle, the seal of his office heavy against his chest, and felt the weight of every gaze in the room settle on him like a physical thing. Seventeen councilors. Three senior auditors. His father in the high seat, face carved from the same gray stone as the walls.

He had not slept. The proof lay in the satchel at his feet, bound in red twine, the same documents he had carried for weeks while the rot spread beneath everything he thought he knew. The engineered failure of Sol's mother. The ward contract Corrin had slipped into the records. The dated offer from House Ashworth to rule against Vane, signed in his father's hand.

His fingers found the clasp of the auditor badge. Cold metal. The Kindling in his veins stirred, a low heat behind his ribs that had nothing to do with flight and everything to do with the moment stretching thin.

"Lord Auditor Ashworth," Councilor Veyne said. Her voice echoed in the vaulted space. "You requested this audience. The floor is yours."

Kael did not look at his father. He did not look at the door where Sol might be standing, where she had been every day since the rupture, silent and watching. He opened the satchel. The twine fell away. He lifted the first sheet, the ink still sharp despite the handling.

"On the eighteenth of Deepwinter, Year 2," he said, and his voice did not shake, "House Corrin submitted a ward contract for the Reckoning of Mara Vane. The contract named a ward who did not exist. The signature of the ward was forged. The auditor who certified it was Valerius Ashworth."

A murmur rose. Councilor Veyne raised a hand. The room fell back into the kind of silence that presses against eardrums.

"The ward trade is controlled by House Corrin," Kael continued. "The records show a payment of twelve thousand crowns from Corrin coffers to the ward registry three days before the Reckoning. The same registry that certified the ward's existence. The same registry that reports to this council." He turned the page. "The offer from House Ashworth to rule against Vane's claim bears my father's seal and the date of the seventeenth. One day before the Reckoning. The terms are explicit. A favorable ruling for Corrin in exchange for the ward contract's certification."

He set the page down. His hands were steady. The heat in his chest burned cleaner now, hotter.

"I swore an oath to the Accord," he said. "To truth before house. To justice before kin. I held this proof for weeks. I did not surface it. That failure is mine. The proof is real. The corruption is real. And I will not carry it another day."

He unclasped the badge. The metal sighed against his palm. He placed it on the stone dais, the sound final as a bell strike.

"I surrender my office," he said. "I surrender my name."

"Kael." His father's voice cracked the air. "You will stop this. Now. You are Ashworth. You are my son. You do not throw away three generations of service for a Vane girl and a grudge against Corrin."

Kael looked up then. The resemblance was brutal. Same jaw. Same eyes the color of storm-clouded slate. Same pride that had built a house on the edge of the world and called it destiny.

"I am not throwing it away," he said. "I am returning it. The house you built stands on a lie. I watched you sign the offer. I watched you certify the ward. I carried the proof and I stayed silent because I was afraid of what it would cost. That silence nearly killed a ward in the square. It nearly killed Sol. It will kill the next one, and the next, until someone stops paying the price."

"The price is order," Valerius said. "The price is the Accord holding. You think Corrin falls alone? You think the ward trade collapses and the Kindling stops burning? There are no wards without the trade. There are no Kindling-wielders who survive without wards. You tear down the system and everyone burns."

"Then we build something that doesn't require burning." The words came from somewhere deeper than calculation. "But not on the backs of women who couldn't afford the cost. Not on the backs of mothers who died because the price was rigged."

"Strike his name," Valerius said to the council. The command landed like a hammer. "Strike the name Ashworth from the auditor rolls. Let the record show that Kael Ashworth has renounced his house, his office, and his oath. Let no door in the Reach open to him. Let no contract bear his witness."

The senior auditors moved. Quills scratched on parchment. The sound was obscenely ordinary.

Kael felt something sever inside him, clean as a blade through silk. No dramatic tear. No shuddering collapse. Just the sudden absence of a weight he had carried since childhood, the name that had opened every door and closed every exit. He was no one now. A man with a satchel of proof and a heat in his chest and no shield against what came next.

He bent. Retrieved the badge. The metal was warm from his palm. He slid it into his coat pocket, not onto his chest.

"The proof remains," he said to the room. "The records are entered. The ward contract is void. Mara Vane's Reckoning was fraudulent. Her daughter's claim stands unless this council finds new grounds to deny it. Grounds that do not come from Corrin gold."

He turned. Walked toward the doors. Did not run. Did not look back at his father's face, though he felt the gaze like a brand between his shoulders.

The doors opened before he reached them. Sol stood there.

She wore the gray wool coat she had worn the night of the chase, the one with the torn sleeve she had never mended. Her face was pale, the freckles standing stark against skin drained of color. Her hands hung loose at her sides. She did not move to let him pass.

He stopped. The heat in his chest pulsed, a rhythm he had learned to read. Three years. Two years. The numbers shifted with every beat, the calculus of survival he had memorized in the dark.

"Sol," he said.

Her name in the chamber. The councilors behind him gone quiet. The auditors still scratching. His father's breathing, ragged and controlled.

"You knew," she said. The words were flat. Not a question. "You knew the ward was forged. You knew my mother's Reckoning was rigged. You knew your father signed the offer. And you held it. For weeks. You held it while I trained. While I begged you to help me understand why the numbers didn't add up. While I thought..." Her voice cracked. "While I thought you were the one person who might actually see me."

"I know."

"Don't." The word whipped. "Don't say you know. Don't make this about what you know. You chose. Every day you chose not to tell me. Every night you came to the ledge and watched me burn years off my life and you chose your name over my mother's truth."

The heat in his chest spiked. He felt the Kindling rise, the familiar rush of power that wanted wings, that wanted to lift him away from this moment and every moment like it. He breathed through it. The technique Sol had taught him, the counting, the anchoring. One. Two. Three. The years burned slower.

"I chose wrong," he said. "Every day. I chose wrong. And I am sorry. That word is not enough. I know it is not enough. But it is the only one I have that does not ask you to forgive me."

Sol's eyes were dry. Her jaw was set. The muscle in her cheek jumped once.

"You stood up there," she said, "and you read it all. Every line. You gave up everything. Your name. Your house. Your father. You burned it all down in front of them. And you did it knowing I was watching."

"I did it because you were watching."

The admission hung between them. The council chamber held its breath.

"Why?" she asked. "Why now? Why not three weeks ago? Why not before the square? Before the ward died?"

"Because I was a coward." The words tasted of ash. "Because I thought I could fix it quietly. Because I thought the system would correct itself if I just found the right lever. Because I was raised to believe that Ashworths do not break things, we repair them. And I forgot that some things cannot be repaired. They can only be broken open."

Sol was silent. The councilors behind him shifted. Someone cleared a throat. The scratching of quills had stopped.

"Is that true?" she asked. "Is that all of it?"

He met her eyes. The gray-green of them, the flecks of gold he had memorized in the dark of the ledge, the way they caught light when she flew. He had no shield left. No badge. No name. No house. Just the truth, raw and bleeding.

"No," he said. "It is not all of it. There is more. The ward trade. The contracts. Who really controls them. I have proof of that too. But it is not in this satchel. And it will cost more than my name to surface it."

Sol's gaze dropped to the satchel at his feet. Then to his pocket where the badge lay. Then back to his face.

"More," she repeated. "There is always more with you."

"There is always more with all of us. The difference is whether we let it stay buried."

She studied him. The silence stretched, filled with the weight of every lie he had told by omission, every night he had held her while the proof burned in his pocket, every time he had said *I will help you* and meant *I will help you on my terms*.

The Kindling in his chest settled into a low hum. The years ticked by, measurable now in heartbeats. He had perhaps forty left. Perhaps fewer. The count had always been approximate, the training only slowing the bleed, never stopping it. He had spent three years of them in the last month alone, the stress and the proximity and the nights he had flown to her ledge and back, burning distance like fuel.

"I don't know if I can trust you," Sol said. "I don't know if trust is a thing that exists between us anymore. Or if it ever did."

"I do not expect trust," he said. "I expect you to watch what I do next. And the next. And the next. That is all I have to offer."

A councilor stood. Veyne, her face impassive. "Lord Ashworth. The record is entered. The name is struck. You are dismissed from this chamber. The council will deliberate on the Vane claim in light of the new evidence. You will be summoned if your testimony is required further. As a private citizen."

Private citizen. The words landed in the space where his identity had been.

Kael inclined his head. "Thank you, Councilor."

He moved toward the door. Sol did not step aside. He stopped before her, close enough to smell the cold air clinging to her coat, the faint scent of pine resin and sweat and something underneath it all that was simply *her*. The Kindling stirred, hungry. He held it down.

"Go," she said. "Before I change my mind about something. Before I decide whether the woman who watched you read those lines is the same woman who stood on the ledge and let you hold her while you lied."

He passed her. The door closed behind him. The corridor stretched ahead, stone and shadow and the distant sound of wind against the cliff face. He walked. His boots on the flags. His breath in the cold. The satchel bumped against his hip with every step.

He had expected the falling to feel like falling. He had expected the void to open beneath him, the name stripped away leaving nothing but the drop. Instead he felt solid ground. Strange. Unfamiliar. The ground of having chosen, finally, the thing that could not be taken back.

The corridor opened onto the balcony over the Reach. The vertical city spread below, lineage-houses clinging to the rock like barnacles, bridges arcing between them, the Kindling-lanterns beginning to wake as Frostveil's short day bled into evening. Somewhere in that maze of stone and rope and light, House Vane clung to its narrow ledge. Somewhere, Sol's sister was training. Somewhere, Corrin's agents were watching.

Kael leaned on the railing. The stone was cold through his coat. The wind tasted of salt and ice and the metallic tang of the Kindling that ran through every bloodline in the Reach, diluted and distant and undeniable.

He was no one's son. No one's auditor. No one's ward. He was a man with a satchel of proof and a heat in his chest and a woman who might never forgive him watching the door of the council chamber, waiting to see what he would burn next.

The Kindling pulsed. Three years. Two years. The count continued.

He pulled the badge from his pocket. Turned it over in his hands. The silver surface caught the last light, the Ashworth sigil worn smooth by generations of thumbs. His grandfather's thumb. His father's. His own.

He dropped it over the railing.

It fell. A flash of silver against the gray stone. A glint against the lanterns waking below. It struck a bridge and bounced, spun, vanished into the shadows between houses.

The sound of its landing was lost to the wind.

Kael watched the space where it had disappeared. His fingers were empty. His chest was warm. The count ticked on.

Behind him, the council chamber door opened. He did not turn. He knew the footfalls. Light. Deliberate. The rhythm of someone who had walked ledges in the dark and learned to measure each step.

" You dropped your badge," Sol said.

Her voice came from close behind. Not the doorway. Right behind him.

He turned. She stood there, the balcony wind tugging at her coat, her hair escaping its braid in wild strands. Her eyes were still dry. Her jaw was still set. But something in the set of her shoulders had shifted, a fraction of a degree, the difference between bracing and leaning.

"It was not mine to keep," he said.

"Neither was the proof. You kept that."

" The proof belonged to the truth. The badge belonged to the lie."

Sol stepped closer. The Kindling in his chest surged, recognize, *reach*, the involuntary pull that had bound them from the first forced proximity in the training courtyard. He felt the years burn, a sudden sharp cost, and knew without looking that she felt the echo of it, the involuntary transfer that had no contract and no consent and no mercy.

He did not step back. He did not shield her. He stood in the wind and the fading light and let the cost take what it would.

"Why are you here?" he asked. "The council dismissed me. The deliberation is closed. You could be with your sister. With your claim. With anything that matters."

Sol's gaze dropped to his chest, where the Kindling burned visible now beneath his coat, a faint glow through the wool. The transfer marks on his skin, the evidence he had hidden and hidden and finally worn into the light.

"Because you dropped the badge," she said. "And you didn't watch it fall. You watched the space where it landed. Like you were checking something."

Kael was silent. The wind tore at his hair. The count in his chest ticked, *thirty-nine, thirty-eight*, the proximity cost steep and relentless.

"What were you checking?" she asked.

"Whether it was caught," he said. "Or whether it hit the bottom."

"And?"

"And I don't know yet. The shadows are too deep. But I will find out. That is what I do now. I find out what happens at the bottom of things."

Sol was close enough now that he could see the individual freckles across her nose. The small scar at her chin from a childhood fall. The way her pupils dilated in the dimming light, the Kindling in her own blood responding to his, the resonance they had never been able to break and never been able to name.

"Then I will come with you," she said.

The words were simple. Final. The kind of declaration that rearranged the architecture of a life.

"You have a Reckoning to prepare for," he said. "A claim to prove. A house to save."

"The claim is proven. The fraud is entered. The house stands or falls on its own merits now. My sister is ready. I have taught her everything I know. Everything except how to stop the burn when someone you..." She stopped. Swallowed. "Everything except that."

"The burn doesn't stop. It only slows. You taught me that."

"I taught you how to count. You taught me how to lie."

The words landed. He deserved them. He deserved every one.

"I did not lie about the proof," he said. "I did not lie about the badge. I did not lie about the name. I lied by silence. I lied by omission. I lied by choosing my safety over your truth. That is the only lie I will ever tell you again, because it is the only one I cannot take back."

Sol was silent for a long moment. The wind howled around the balcony, tearing at the lanterns on the bridges below. Somewhere a Kindling-winged messenger passed, a streak of fire against the darkening sky.

"Then tell me the rest," she said. "The more you mentioned. The ward trade. Who controls it. All of it. Tell me now, here, in the wind, where no one can hear and no one can stop you. Tell me and I will decide if the woman who watched you read those lines can become the woman who stands beside you while you burn the rest down."

Kael looked at her. Really looked. The gray-green eyes. The stubborn jaw. The hands that had held blades and ledgers and his face in the dark. The woman who had flown into a storm to save a ward she didn't know and burned three years doing it. The woman who had watched him choose silence and then watched him choose truth and was still standing here, asking for more.

He thought of the proof in the satchel. The deeper records. The name at the top of the ward registry that was not Corrin. The name that was older than the Accord, older than the Reach, older than the Kindling itself. The name that meant the trade was not a trade at all. It was a harvest.

He thought of the cost. The years. The count that had dropped from forty to thirty-eight in the space of this conversation. The knowledge that every moment near her burned him faster, and every moment away burned him slower but colder.

He thought of his father's face in the council chamber. The pride. The fear. The calculation that had built a house on corruption and called it order.

He thought of the badge falling. The shadows. The bottom he could not see.

"All right," he said. "All of it. But not here. The walls have ears even in the wind. There is a place. An old aerie on the north face. Abandoned since the Winter Collapse. No wards. No listeners. Just stone and sky."

"Then we go there. Now."

"Now. Tonight. The climb is hard. The ledges are slick with frost. You will burn years."

"I burn years breathing. Lead the way, private citizen."

The title landed between them, a joke and a wound and a promise all at once. Kael felt something in his chest loosen, a knot he had tied so long ago he had forgotten the rope.

"Kael," he said. "Just Kael."

"Kael," she repeated. The name sounded different in her mouth. Older. Heavier. Like a stone set into a foundation. "Lead the way, Kael."

He turned. The north face waited, a vertical labyrinth of ice and stone and memory. The satchel weighed against his hip. The Kindling burned in his veins, a furnace he had learned to feed with truth instead of silence.

Behind him, Sol followed. Her boots on the stone. Her breath in the cold. The sound of someone choosing, finally, the same dangerous path.

The wind took the rest.

---

<!-- chapter_date: 25 Frostveil, Year 3 of the Reckoning Accord -->

The lower market smelled of wet iron and rotting kelp. Kael moved through the press of bodies with his ledger tucked against his chest, the Auditor's seal visible on the leather. Three days since he had submitted the Corrin ledger extracts to the Central Archive. Three days since his father's messenger had found him in the records room with a summons he had burned rather than answered.

He should have expected the response to be faster than official channels.

The alley behind the salt-merchant stalls narrowed to a slit between two lineage-house foundations. Kael's boot struck something soft. He looked down. A rat, crushed flat. The market's refuse chute emptied here, a black throat swallowing waste from the levels above.

"Ashworth."

The voice came from above. Kael tilted his head back. Three figures on the catwalk twenty feet up. Corrin livery stitched dark on dark at their collars. No masks. They did not need masks in the lower market.

"Inquisitor Vane sends his regards," the lead figure said. "And a warning. The Archive copy is... incomplete."

Kael's hand drifted to the baton at his belt. Not a weapon. A badge of office. Useless against three Kindling-touched blades.

"Inquisitor Corrin," Kael corrected. "Vane holds no inquisitorial seat."

The lead figure smiled. "Not for long."

They dropped.

Kael moved. Not up. The catwalk was theirs. Not back. The alley mouth had filled with two more silhouettes. He went down, into the refuse chute.

The drop was fifteen feet. He hit sludge and something that cracked beneath his shoulder. Bone, perhaps. Old waste compacted into strata. The stench gagged him. He scrambled up, boots slipping on slime-slick stone, and found a maintenance hatch rusted shut.

A blade punched through the metal inches from his fingers. Daylight speared the darkness.

"Cornered," the lead assassin called down. "Like the rat you are."

Kael pressed his back to the curved wall. His marks burned hot along his forearm, the Kindling-scars pulsing in time with his heart. Two years since the first transfer. Two years since the night chase across the ledges when Sol's desperation had seared itself into his flesh without permission.

He had reported neither incident. The auditor's code demanded immediate declaration of any Kindling contact. He had lied in his quarterly filings. He had lied to his father. He had lied to the Reckoning Court.

The hatch shuddered. Metal screamed. They were prying it open from above.

Then the air changed.

A pressure dropped into the chute like a stone into still water. Kael's ears popped. The hair on his arms stood vertical. The marks on his forearm flared white-hot, visible through his shirt sleeve.

Sol.

She landed beside him in a crouch, boots silent on the sludge. Her face was streaked with soot. A cut bisected her left eyebrow, clotting dark. Kindling fire crawled along her fingers, not the controlled brushstroke of training but a wild corona that licked at her wrists.

She did not look at him. Her eyes tracked the hatch above.

"Three above," she said. "Two at the mouth. Five total."

"Corrin inquisitors."

"Corrin killers." She flexed her hands. The fire steadied, drawing into a controlled sheath along her forearms. "The ventilation shaft. Third junction. It connects to the old smelter flue."

"Blocked since the collapse."

"Cleared last week. I checked."

She moved before he could answer. Straight up the chute wall, fingers finding purchase in mortar gaps too shallow for a normal climb. Kindling enhanced grip. He had read the theory. Seeing it in practice was different.

Kael followed.

His boots found the same holds. His fingers burned with cold sludge and older grime. The hatch exploded outward behind them as they reached the junction. A cross-tunnel of brick and rusted iron, wide enough to walk doubled over. The ventilation shaft yawned to the left, a square throat swallowing darkness.

"Go," Sol said. She did not turn.

Kael went.

The shaft climbed at a steep angle. Rungs bolted into brick, half eaten by salt air. He climbed with his back to the void, Sol's boots clanging above him. The marks on his arm throbbed. Each pulse sent heat radiating toward his elbow, toward his shoulder.

Two years. The first transfer had taken two. The night chase had taken three more. The marks had not faded. They accumulated.

Behind them, shouts echoed. Metal on metal. The assassins entering the junction.

"Faster," Sol called down. Her voice carried strain.

Kael's lungs burned. His arms trembled. He had not climbed like this in years. Not since the auditor's trials, when physical fitness was tested alongside legal knowledge. He had passed. Barely.

A rung gave way. His foot slipped. He caught himself on the next rung down, shoulder jarred, breath knocked loose.

Sol's boot appeared beside his face. She had descended two rungs. Her hand clamped onto his wrist. Not a grip. A lock. Kindling fire flowed from her palm into his forearm, hot and sharp.

"Breathe," she said.

The fire did not burn. It seeped into the marks, into the hunger that lived beneath them. For a heartbeat the pain vanished. Then it returned, changed. Duller. Contained.

"Two years," she said. "Maybe three. The transfer took more than I intended."

Kael stared at her hand on his wrist. The marks glowed through her fingers, white light bleeding between them.

"You feel it," she said. Not a question.

"Yes."

"Good." She released him and climbed on. "Keep climbing."

He climbed.

The shaft opened onto a platform. The old smelter flue gaped ahead, a tunnel of blackened brick large enough for a cart. Sol dropped onto the platform and turned, offering a hand down.

Kael took it. Her palm was callused, hot. The marks on his arm flared again as their skin contacted. Not a transfer. A resonance. Like striking a tuning fork beside another tuned to the same note.

He swung up beside her. The platform shuddered. Dust sifted from the ceiling.

"They're in the shaft," Sol said. "Two minutes. Maybe three."

She led him into the flue. The air grew hotter. Sulfur and old ash coated the walls. Side tunnels branched at intervals, sealed with brick and warning signs.

"Where does this exit?" Kael asked.

"Upper forge district. Abandoned since the gas pocket killed the shift crew in Year One."

"Sealed by Court order."

"Sealed by Corrin order. The Court ratified it." She did not slow. "There's a maintenance hatch at the thirty-yard mark. Rust-sealed. I can breach it."

"And if you can't?"

"Then we fight in the open. Better than fighting in a tube."

The flue stretched straight and featureless. Their boots raised clouds of ash. Kael's breathing steadied. The marks on his arm had settled to a dull throb. Two years. The knowledge settled in his chest like a stone in still water.

Two years of his life. Gone into the dark.

He had known the cost was real. He had read the medical reports, the auditor case studies, the Corrin propaganda about ward contracts protecting both parties. He had not known the hunger.

It was not pain. Pain was sharp. This was a hollow place behind the ribs, a mouth opening in the dark, waiting. When Sol's fire had touched him, the mouth had snapped shut. Satisfied. For a moment.

Now it opened again.

"What is it?" Sol asked. She had glanced back. Her eyes caught the faint light from the shaft behind them.

"What?"

"The hunger. You're feeling it."

Kael almost denied it. The auditor's instinct: conceal weakness, control information. The words died in his throat.

"Yes."

Sol's face did not change. She faced forward. "It's the Kindling recognizing a compatible vessel. The marks create a... channel. The more transfers, the wider the channel. The hunger grows."

"Can it be closed?"

"No." She spoke flatly. "Only managed. Wards manage it by contract. Consent. Controlled burns on a schedule. You..." She hesitated. "You have no contract. No schedule. The hunger feeds on proximity."

"On you."

"On the source. The Kindling wants what it gave. It pulls."

Ahead, the flue widened. A circular hatch dominated the ceiling, its iron ring fused with rust. Sol stopped beneath it. She pressed her palm flat against the metal.

Fire bloomed.

Not the controlled sheath. A white lance of heat that screamed against the iron. The metal groaned, cherry-red spreading from her touch. Rivets popped. The hatch sagged.

Sol gritted her teeth. Her free hand clenched at her side. The marks on her own arms, faint tracery visible at her cuffs, pulsed in counterpoint to Kael's.

The hatch tore free. It crashed into the flue above, sending a cascade of brick dust and cold air.

Sol sagged. Kael caught her elbow before she could fall.

The contact sent a jolt through both of them. His marks flared. Hers answered. The hunger in his chest snapped shut again, then reopened wider. Deeper.

"Two years," she breathed. "Maybe three. Each time."

She pulled away. The connection severed. Cold air rushed in where her warmth had been.

"Go," she said. "I'll follow."

Kael climbed through the hatch into a corridor of fitted stone. Upper forge district. Abandoned machinery loomed in shadows. A ladder ascended to a street grate sealed with wax and the Corrin sigil.

He waited at the top. Sol emerged behind him, moving slower now. The Kindling fire had guttered to embers along her forearms. Sweat darkened her hairline.

The grate yielded to a kick. Night air hit them. Cold. Clean. Stars overhead, sharp as blown glass.

They stood in a narrow alley between silent forges. The Reach's great chain curved overhead, the lineage-houses hanging like lanterns from its links. House Ashworth's spire caught starlight on its warding crystals. House Corrin's bulk blotted out the stars to the east.

Kael's breath fogged. His arm throbbed. Two years. The knowledge was physical now, not abstract. A weight in his bones.

"Your father's summons," Sol said quietly. "You burned it."

"Yes."

"Why?"

Kael looked at her. The cut on her eyebrow had reopened, a thin trickle tracing her temple. Her eyes held no accusation. No demand. Only a question offered freely.

"Because it was an order to rule against your house. Dated yesterday. Sealed with his signet." Kael's voice was rough. "Because I have the proof of what Corrin did to your mother. Because I hid it from you for weeks. Because every day I delay, the Reckoning approaches and I have done nothing."

Sol wiped blood from her temple. "You submitted the ledger extracts to the Archive."

"Three days ago. Corrin's response was tonight."

"Then you're doing something."

"Am I?"

She studied him. The hunger in his chest pulsed. Her marks answered, a silent conversation beneath skin.

"The repair isn't a single act," she said. "It's every act after. You know that."

"I know."

"Then climb."

She turned toward the great chain's nearest anchor point. A maintenance ladder climbed the cliff face, bolted into living rock. The legitimate path. The watched path.

Kael followed.

Halfway up, his boot slipped on frost-slick iron. He caught himself. The marks burned. Below, in the alley, figures emerged from the forge shadows. Corrin livery. More than five.

They had not lost the pursuit. They had only delayed it.

"Contact," Sol called down. "Six. Maybe eight."

"Archers?"

"Two. Crossbows. The rest blades."

Kael climbed faster. The hunger in his chest gnawed. Each rung brought him closer to Sol above him, closer to the source. The marks on his arm grew hotter. The pull strengthened.

He understood now why wards were contracted. Why the trade existed. The hunger was not metaphor. It was a living thing, a parasite born of stolen years, feeding on proximity to its maker.

And Sol carried the source.

She reached the anchor platform. Turned. Extended a hand down the final ten feet.

"Come on."

Kael reached. His fingers brushed hers. Fire jumped the gap.

Not a transfer. A sharing. The hunger in him met the fire in her and for a heartbeat they burned together, two candles fed from the same wax. The years lost settled into place. Two years. Three. Five. Seven. The count stabilized.

He hauled himself onto the platform. Sol did not release his hand.

Below, crossbow bolts struck iron. Sparks showered into the dark.

"Move," she said.

They ran along the anchor platform to the chain itself. The great links, each the width of a man's torso, connected to a winch house carved into the cliff. The winch house was Ashworth territory. Neutral ground by ancient treaty.

Corrin assassins would not follow onto Ashworth steel. Not openly.

Kael's boots rang on the chain. Sol matched his pace. The wind tore at them, cold and clean. The marks on his arm pulsed in rhythm with his strides.

Two years. The number settled into his bones.

He had spent twenty-seven years building a career on precision. On the auditor's creed: truth measured, recorded, reported. He had lied in his filings. He had concealed evidence. He had burned his father's summons.

And tonight, hanging from a chain above the abyss, holding the hand of the woman he had failed, he felt the Kindling's hunger for the first time not as a curse visited upon him but as a burden they carried together.

The winch house loomed. Ashworth guards stood at the door, halberds leveled. They lowered weapons as Kael approached, recognizing the Auditor's seal.

"Lord Auditor," the sergeant said. "Your father waits inside."

Kael released Sol's hand. The hunger opened wide in his chest, a mouth gaping in the dark.

"Tell him I'm coming," Kael said. "After I see the Archive copy verified. The Corrin ledger extracts. I need confirmation they match what I submitted."

The sergeant hesitated. "Lord Valerius ordered—"

"I know what he ordered." Kael's voice cut the wind. "I also know what the law requires. The Archive verification is my right as sitting auditor. Inform him I'll report to him after."

The sergeant nodded sharply. "As you command, Lord Auditor."

Kael turned to Sol. She stood at the chain's edge, wind whipping her hair. The embers on her forearms had died. She looked small against the night. She looked infinite.

"Tomorrow," she said. "The Archive opens at sixth bell. I'll be in the public gallery."

"Sol."

She met his eyes. "Tomorrow."

He watched her descend the maintenance ladder on the far side of the platform, moving into Corrin territory, into the dark between houses. The hunger in his chest screamed at the distance. Two years. Maybe three. The cost of proximity paid in instalments.

He turned toward the winch house. Toward his father. Toward the proof he had buried and now must exhume.

The Reckoning was coming. The date was fixed. The countdown continued.

And for the first time since the night chase, Kael Ashworth did not walk alone into the dark.

---

<!-- chapter_date: 26 Frostveil, Year 3 of the Reckoning Accord -->

The wind came off the Grey Spine at dawn, cold enough to crack the skin on Sol's knuckles. She stood on the western ledge of House Vane's aerie, the Reckoning crystal cold against her palm where she had wedged it into the mortar crack. Three days. Seventy-two hours before the auditor's bench would fill, before the lineage-houses would send their witnesses to the central plaza, before Corrin's law would either break or harden into something permanent.

Kael approached from the stairwell, his breath visible in the air. He carried a satchel slung across his chest, the leather worn at the corners. His auditor's sigil had been scratched from the clasp. A deliberate choice, or maybe just the only leather he had left after House Ashworth froze his accounts.

"You're up early," he said.
